# what are your thoughts on the approach to partial language-specific handling of colonel processing for per-file routing ```
# i think some of these issues can be alleviated by putting the onus on compiling the chain plan before chain executor sees it

That's the right inversion, and it goes further than alleviating — it takes chain-executor's diff to **zero**.

## The move

`Compile-Plan` stops emitting one plan and emits a **plan family**: N fully-flat, fully-bound step lists, one per route, plus a default. The chain the caller writes is a *template* with `@strip` as a hole; compiling instantiates it once per file class and splices the hole shut:

```
template:    file-read → @strip → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta

powershell:  file-read → rs-psstrip → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta
python:      file-read → rs.py.strip → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta
default:     file-read → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta          ← hole spliced out
```

Every variant is an ordinary flat chain of `@{Key; Fn; Config}` — the exact shape chain-executor already consumes. It never learns what an extension is.

## What that deletes

- **The nested halt check.** No sub-chain exists; a language's two processors are peers in a flat list, so the existing halt-at-top-of-loop is correct again.
- **The multi-processor array as a special case.** `"Processors": ["rs.ps.strip","rs.ps.dedoc"]` is a compile-time splice of two steps. Declaration order is preserved by construction — no new bind shape, no ordered-array semantics to document at runtime.
- **`Resolve-BagExtension`.** Extension resolution happens colonel-side where `$Item.Extension` sits on the descriptor and there's no ISS boundary to cross. bag-helpers is untouched.
- **The caution blind spot** — and it inverts into coverage. "content_meta must be last," "chain omits rs-whitespace" now run per compiled variant, which is strictly more than today's single check. `Only`-scoping and corpus-pruning are filters on the route table before compilation, so they cost nothing.
- **`ConfigEcho` honesty**, again improved: echo the variant chains verbatim. A reading agent gets `powershell: file-read → rs-psstrip → …` / `default: file-read → …`, which is literally what ran per file class.

One correction to what I said earlier: there's no separate serial branch — [colonel.v2:531](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:531) is the single marshalling point, so the risk there was a silent field-drop, not a serial/parallel divergence. Under this design it mostly evaporates anyway, since the step shape stays `@{Key;Fn;Config}` and it's the *plan argument* that changes — a visible signature change that fails loudly.

## Where routing lands

Slice time, as a third parallel array alongside the two that exist. Slicing is round-robin at [colonel.v2:445](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:445) with `$OrderedOut[$idx]` preserving order, so it's symmetric:

```powershell
$sliceItems[$slot].Add($Items[$i])
$sliceIdxs[$slot].Add($i)
$slicePlans[$slot].Add($extToVariant[$ext] ?? 'default')    # variant id, resolved here
```

The worker then does `$plan = $Family[$MyPlanKeys[$i]]` — an index lookup, no extension logic, no conditionals. The colonel decides; the worker obeys. That's the full expression of putting the onus on compilation.

Variant identity should be per *route*, not per extension — `.ps1` and `.psm1` share one compiled list, so 20 languages is 21 variants, each compiled once with shared config object references. Marshal the family as plain nested hashtables, keeping the deliberate minimal-shape discipline that's already at :531.

## The one thing this forecloses

Routing is chosen before `file-read` runs, so it can never depend on **content** — no shebang sniffing for extensionless files (`Makefile`, `#!/usr/bin/env nu` with no extension). Extension-only routing was already the v1 call, so nothing is lost today, but it's now structural rather than incidental.

The escape hatch, if you ever want it: hoist `file-read` out of the variants into a fixed prologue, and route on the post-read bag. That makes it a two-wave dispatch in `Invoke-Plan` rather than one, which is a real restructure — worth knowing the shape exists, not worth building for it now. It doesn't change any decision in front of you.

## What's actually left to decide

The routing file's own questions survive unchanged — `processors/routing.json` vs `configs/`, `Strip` as a named table, `.psd1`, dot-normalization on load, hard-error-on-malformed. Those are about the *file*, not the mechanism.

The mechanism adds one: **does `@strip` splice to nothing in the default variant, or does an unrouted file get no chain at all?** Splicing to nothing is right — a `.md` file should still get `file-read → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta`; it just isn't stripped. But that means the default variant always exists and is the template minus every hole, which is worth stating as an invariant rather than falling out by accident.

---

# an unrouted file doesn't get any routed treatment. no stripper or otherwise filetype specific -> doesn't get stripped or otherwise, but everything else in the same order

Settled, and it closes into a general rule rather than a special case for stripping:

**A template is a flat chain that may contain holes. Compiling instantiates it per route: each hole is replaced by that route's processors in declared order; the default variant is the template with every hole removed. Non-hole steps keep their relative order in every variant.**

Which gives you, for free:

- **Position is the caller's.** `-Processors 'rs-indent','@strip','rs-whitespace'` strips after indent. The hole isn't pinned to a slot in the pipeline — it's wherever it's written. `-StripComments` is then just "the template contains `@strip`," inserted after `file-read` if the caller didn't place it themselves, and a no-op if they did. No exclusivity rule against `-Processors` needed.
- **More than one hole works with no new machinery.** When `tp-perplexity` or a future `@parse` wants routed treatment, it's a second table in the routing file and a second hole in the template. Each splices independently; the default variant drops both.
- **Variants differ in length, so nothing may key on step index.** No code does today, but the manifest echo is the obvious future temptation — key it by variant.

One collision worth a caution rather than an error: a route naming a processor the template already lists explicitly compiles it twice in that variant. Harmless for idempotent ops, silently doubled work otherwise.

That's the mechanism fully closed. What's left is all file-level, and my recommendation on each:

| | Recommend |
|---|---|
| Location | `processors/routing.json` — keeps `configs/` at exactly one file per processor key, mechanically checkable |
| Top-level | Named table (`"Strip": {...}`) — one line now, room for `"Parse"` later |
| Route keys | Language ids, extensions dot-less in the file, normalized to lowercase-with-dot on load |
| `Processors` entries | Processor **keys**, not filenames (`"rs-psstrip"`, later `"rs.ps.strip"`) |
| `.psd1` | Include it — you can drop it from one array later if manifest comments turn out to matter |
| Malformed routing file | Hard error, unlike `configs/<key>.json`'s warn-and-default |

Want me to write this up as `docs/routing-and-templates.md` first so the target shape is on paper before code, or go straight to implementing the compile-time split with `rs-psstrip`/`rs-csstrip` as the only two routes?

# well, there are declared stages, with order determined canonically internally based on mutation / not mutation, but ordering even within mutation. stripping has a place. some other type specific might sit somewhere else, in theory. each language-typed case gets a $meta-token assigned to its place ($strip, $whatever, $whatever else). They get declared in the order, and cases that don't resolve under a $meta-token have that $step removed from their chain

Then the canon replaces caller positioning entirely, and it's worth naming *why* each edge exists — because that's what makes the next token's placement derivable instead of a judgment call:

**Stage A must follow stage B if B can produce output violating A's postcondition.**

Every edge you have falls out of that. Stripping emits trailing whitespace and blank runs → violates whitespace-tidy's postcondition → tidy runs after strip. Every mutator invalidates a measurement → measure runs last. Indent's common-indent calculation changes once standalone comment lines are gone → indent runs after strip. None of that is taste, and when `$whatever-else` shows up you place it by asking which existing postconditions it can break, not by arguing about pipelines.

There's live evidence the canon exists but isn't enforced: [rs-csstrip.ps1:31](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/rs-csstrip.ps1:31), [rs-whitespace.ps1:35](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/rs-whitespace.ps1:35), and [tp-perplexity.ps1:48](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/tp-perplexity.ps1:48) each defensively re-normalize line endings, because no one can currently rely on running after a stage that guarantees it. Once the spine is declared, that's either deletable or a sign that line-ending canonicalization is its own early stage rather than an op inside whitespace-tidy.

## The spine is an artifact, not a caller argument

```json
{
  "Stages": [
    { "Stage": "read",       "Processor": "file-read" },
    { "Stage": "$parse",     "Routed": "Parse" },
    { "Stage": "$strip",     "Routed": "Strip" },
    { "Stage": "indent",     "Processor": "rs-indent" },
    { "Stage": "whitespace", "Processor": "rs-whitespace" },
    { "Stage": "measure",    "Processor": "rs-content_meta" }
  ],
  "Strip": {
    "powershell": { "Extensions": ["ps1","psm1","psd1"], "Processors": ["rs-psstrip"] },
    "csharp":     { "Extensions": ["cs","csx"],          "Processors": ["rs-csstrip"] }
  }
}
```

Spine and routing tables belong in the same file: token ids have to agree across them, and splitting them means a rename can silently orphan a stage.

Callers then **enable stages, never order them**. `-StripComments` turns on `$strip`. Compilation walks the spine once per file class, keeps enabled stages, resolves routed ones, drops tokens that don't resolve for that class.

## The fork this forces

`-Processors` as an ordered caller-supplied chain and a canonical spine can't both be authoritative. Either it becomes a **set** — the caller names participants, the compiler orders them, and array position is meaningless (or a validation error when it contradicts canon) — or it stays a literal sequence that bypasses the spine.

I'd take both, explicitly separated: `-Processors` becomes a set under the canon, and a distinct flag means "take my order verbatim, cautions only." That keeps reposnapshot usable as an instrument for deliberately violating its own invariants, which you will want, without letting a plain call do it by accident. Name that flag carefully — `-OrderStrict` is already taken by bin packing at [rs.core.user.ps1:97](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:97).

That also cleanly disposes of the three soft cautions at [rs.core.user.ps1:311](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:311): under the canon they become compiler guarantees and stop being printed at all; under the verbatim flag they're exactly the right warnings and stay.

## Two consequences of having more than one token

**Variant identity is the tuple of resolutions, not one token's.** A `.md` file might resolve `$parse` and not `$strip`; a `.ps1` the reverse. Variants key on the whole tuple — bounded by the tuples actually present in the corpus, not the product of the tables, but it's the thing that surprises you when the second token lands.

**Tokens are the unit of echo.** In the manifest, `$strip → rs-psstrip` reads differently from a fixed stage, and that distinction is what tells a reading agent "this position was type-routed, and for this file it resolved to nothing" versus "this stage doesn't exist in this run."

One practical note on the sigil: `'$strip'` is literal in single quotes and in JSON, but `"$strip"` in PowerShell double quotes interpolates to an empty string. Silent, and exactly the kind of thing that shows up as a mysterious missing stage.

Ready to write this up as the design doc — spine, derivation rule, routing tables, compilation, variant tuples — or do you want to settle the `-Processors` set-vs-sequence call first, since it changes what the doc says about the caller surface?

Let me ground a couple of things in the current code before weighing in — mainly the processor inventory and how `-Processors` is consumed today.
One more quick check — how the processor manifest is built, since that's where any stage/class metadata would have to hang:
The approach is sound, and the trajectory across the three exchanges moved in the right direction — each turn pushed more decision-making out of runtime and into compilation. My assessment, having checked it against the current code:

**The partiality boundary lands in the right place.** Language-specificity ends up as *data* (routing tables keyed by language id), consumed once at compile time. The colonel learns only "which variant key does this extension map to," resolved at slice time where `$Item.Extension` is already in hand; the worker does an index lookup; chain-executor and bag-helpers never learn extensions exist. Adding a language becomes a routing row plus a processor file, with zero code diff in the dispatch machinery. That's the correct blast radius, and it's the strongest property of the design — worth stating as an invariant in the doc: *no component below plan compilation may branch on file type.*

**The spine is the strongest single idea, because it's testable.** Compilation is a pure function: (spine, routing tables, corpus extension set) → plan family. That means the whole mechanism can be pinned by unit tests with no dispatch, no runspaces, no filesystem — which matters given the project doctrine that tests are the only enforcement. The postcondition-derivation rule ("A follows B if B can violate A's postcondition") should live in the doc as prose, but its *consequences* should live in a test that asserts the spine invariants (measure last, strip precedes whitespace-tidy, every variant's non-hole steps in template order). A runtime validator would be policing; a test is the house style.

**The live gap: the canon only covers processors it names.** This is the thing I'd settle before writing the doc. The processor manifest is pure filename discovery — [rs.core.user.ps1:221](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:221) globs `processors/*.ps1` and records nothing but the path — and `tp-perplexity.ps1` already exists outside the spine's vocabulary. Under "`-Processors` is a set, the compiler orders it," the compiler needs a derivable position for *any* nameable processor, and for tp-perplexity it has none: nothing anywhere declares whether a processor mutates or measures, or which postconditions it can break. So the set-vs-sequence fork and the "where does stage classification live" question are actually one decision, not two. The options are roughly: each processor declares its stage/class (a `Stage` field in `configs/<key>.json`, or in the routing file's spine itself), or unclassified processors are only legal under the verbatim flag. I'd lean toward declaration-in-config — it keeps the one-file-per-key convention doing work — but either way the doc can't specify the caller surface until this is picked, which answers the transcript's closing question: settle this first, then write the doc.

**One interaction to flag in the doc: line endings and byte accounting.** The observation that three processors defensively re-normalize line endings is the best empirical evidence the canon is needed. But the tempting resolution — make LF a postcondition of the read stage — changes byte semantics: content measured downstream would diverge from on-disk bytes, and SizeBytes vs SpanBytes is already a historically confused distinction in this codebase. Wherever canonicalization lands, its stage's postcondition should state explicitly which byte accounting survives it. That's exactly the kind of thing the spine format is *for*, so it's an argument for the design, but it needs to be written down rather than discovered.

**Smaller notes.** The `$strip` interpolation hazard dissolves if you adopt a discipline rather than a warning: tokens exist only as data read from the routing file, never as literals in PowerShell source — then there's no double-quoted context to interpolate in. And the enabled-vs-unresolved distinction (stage disabled for the run vs. stage present but resolving to nothing for this file class) is two different manifest stories; make that an explicit invariant of the ConfigEcho format, since a reading agent is the consumer and that distinction is precisely what it needs to reconstruct what ran.

Net: the mechanism is closed and I wouldn't reopen any of it. The remaining open item is genuinely just the classification question — where a processor's stage membership is declared — and it's load-bearing for both the `-Processors` fork and the doc's caller-surface section.

# well part of the original design so far explains the redundancy across steps, which i've explained previously ad nauseum, which is that preconditions for a given stage in a pipeline that is configurable to include different combinations of stages, each stage covers its own bases such that its preconditions are met . that's white some rs-whitespace operations happen redundantly across different processors. but the nominal mode ialmost always going to be a canonical sequence of pipeline stages and what im trying to codify here is a design that naturally handles routing for cases where language-specific treatment by language specific processors is required in a heterogeneous set. the modular design should be that for each ingested file, a specific plan is resolved by the compiler, and then chain executor dutifully executes business as usual based on how its built, its jsut that the loop provided for a given ingest item might differ. it might be helpful to consider the distinct set of processor chains that resolve for a given ingestion set where langauge specific processors are involved, such as a heterogeneous ingestion of python and powershell, where user toggles -StripComments and specifies otherwise language agnostic processors. In this case there might be many files but if the configuration only selects .py and .ps1 files then only two distinct chains get resolved, and perhaps this might inform a different provisioning strategy in the colonel's dispatch strategy, or perhaps not, because after we have resolved the distinct chains, we still have a flat list of files to be processed and that can still be done round-robin, only now each job gets its assigned chain processor. maybe this means colonel spinning up runspaces that have a superset of dependencies for all distinct chains that it needs to run, maybe it means spinning up distinct runspaces with dependencies per distinct processor chain. I favor the former here. the case where theres a third filetype in the ingestion set that isn't touched by a language specific processor is simply a 3rd distinct processor chain that happens to have 1 or more steps omitted compared to the ones that do. the colonel and processor architecture isn't totally prepared for this because it's assume monolithic planning and processor chains but the adaptation really shouldn't be very hard

Let me look at the actual dispatch shapes in the colonel before responding — the slicing region, the marshalling point, and where processor scripts get loaded into runspaces — so the "adaptation shouldn't be hard" claim gets checked against the real seams.
Having now read the dispatch path end to end, the architecture is better prepared for this than "not totally prepared" suggests — the superset option you favor is essentially already the shape of the ISS layer, and the monolithic assumption is confined to exactly three sites.

**The ISS is already a superset mechanism.** `Compile-Plan` registers processor bodies as functions into one `InitialSessionState`, and `Invoke-Plan` creates a single `RunspacePool` from it at [colonel.v2:453](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:453) — every runspace in the pool already receives every registered function. Distinct-runspaces-per-chain would require multiple pools or per-runspace session states, which is a genuine restructure of the pool layer for no benefit. The superset variant is a one-line semantic change: `$referencedKeys` becomes the union of keys across all variants instead of one chain's keys. Function registration is cheap; the pool stays singular; nothing about budget resolution or worker lifetime changes.

**The monolithic-plan assumption lives in three places, and each adaptation is mechanical.** The Plan object's `Steps` becomes a `Variants` table (variant-key → step list). The single marshal at [colonel.v2:531](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:531) becomes a keyed family of the same `@{Key; Fn; Config}` shapes. The slicing loop at [colonel.v2:444](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:444) grows one parallel array alongside `$sliceItems`/`$sliceIdxs` — the pattern is already there, it's a third `.Add()`. The worker builds its `$plans` lookup table once at startup instead of one `$plan`, and the per-item body changes from `Plan = $plan` to `Plan = $plans[$MyVariantKeys[$i]]`. Chain-executor, bag-helpers, stream harvesting, and the index-stable `$OrderedOut` envelope are all untouched. So yes — the adaptation is genuinely small, and I'd add: the two-call API surface survives intact if the routing map (extension → variant key) is carried *inside* the frozen Plan. Compile-Plan emits it; Invoke-Plan does the lookup at slice time; the caller still passes just Items and Plan.

**On whether the distinct-chain set should inform provisioning: no, and for a positive reason, not just parsimony.** Round-robin over the flat list gives every worker a statistically representative mix of variants, so worker wall-times stay balanced even when variants have very different costs — a strip-heavy `.ps1` chain versus the shorter default chain. Partitioning dispatch by chain would concentrate the expensive variant into fewer workers and reintroduce exactly the load imbalance the round-robin exists to prevent. The variant count being small (two or three distinct chains for your example corpus) is a property of the *plan family*, and it's the right place for that smallness to live: compile few, dispatch flat, assign per item.

**The precondition-self-sufficiency discipline is what makes all of this safe — it's load-bearing for routing, not redundancy to clean up.** I'll retract the implication from the earlier discussion that the defensive re-normalization becomes deletable once the spine exists. Under your frame the contract has two layers, and they do different jobs: each stage re-establishes its own preconditions, which guarantees *correctness* for any stage combination — including every variant the compiler can produce, holes spliced out, in a heterogeneous set — with no per-variant analysis needed. The canon then orders the *nominal* path for quality and non-waste. That layering is precisely why the default variant "just works" for the third untouched filetype: no downstream stage depends on the omitted stage having run. Worth stating in the design doc as the reason variant proliferation is cheap, because it's the invariant that must not erode — the day a processor starts assuming its canonical predecessor ran, per-file routing silently breaks for the variants that omit it.

One small decision this surfaces, since Compile-Plan currently validates only referenced keys: with a family, I'd split validation from binding. Validate every route in the routing file unconditionally — its processor keys must exist in the manifest, hard error otherwise, consistent with the settled malformed-file posture — but compile and register into the ISS only the variants whose tuples the ingestion set actually contains. That way a broken routing row for a language absent from today's corpus fails today, not the first time someone ingests that language, while the ISS stays as lean as the corpus allows.

# well if in the hypothetical situation with a heterogeneous ingest of python, powershell and some language that doesn't have a strip processor implemented, say typescript at this moment, then typescript still gets ingested, it just doesn't have its comments stripped, and the processor chain that is compiled for typescript files in that run simply do not include a processor step, e.g. a chain of length one less than the others

Exactly — that's the settled default-variant semantics doing its job in the concrete case, and it's worth noting the mechanism doesn't even treat TypeScript specially: "language we know but haven't written a stripper for" and "extension no route has ever heard of" are the same bucket. Both fall through to the template-minus-holes variant. There's no language registry beyond the routing tables themselves, so "unimplemented" isn't a state the compiler knows about — and it doesn't need to be, because the day `rs.ts.strip` lands, the entire change is one routing row and TypeScript files leave the default bucket. That run compiles four variants instead of three with zero code diff. That's the payoff of the whole design in one move.

Two things this scenario sharpens:

**The echo has to distinguish "enabled but unresolved" from "disabled."** In this run, `$strip` was *on* — the user toggled `-StripComments` — and for TypeScript it resolved to nothing. A reading agent looking at the payload needs to know that TypeScript comments are present because no resolver existed, not because stripping was off for the run. Per-variant echo carries this naturally if each variant reports its token resolutions (`$strip → rs-psstrip` / `$strip → ∅`), but it's worth pinning as a requirement of the echo format rather than something that happens to fall out: the run-level story is "stripping enabled," the variant-level story is "resolved to nothing here," and both must be legible.

**Chains of unequal length now coexist within a single run**, which upgrades the earlier "nothing may key on step index" note from a caution about future code to an invariant that's violated on day one if anyone slips. Step identity in bags, provenance, and any per-step accounting must be by processor key (which it is today), and any future manifest structure that tabulates per-step data across files has to key by variant, never by position. Cheap to assert in the compile-plan tests: the family for a mixed corpus contains variants of differing lengths, and the executor envelope stays index-stable regardless.

Neither of those changes the mechanism — they're both constraints on how the run reports itself. The compilation story for your three-language hypothetical is fully covered by what's already settled: three variants, flat round-robin, per-item variant key, TypeScript's chain one step shorter and correct by the self-sufficiency discipline.

# well i think chain iterator can still use indices, but when there turns out to be a step taht isn't applicable in the compilation of a given chain (e.g. no strip processor typescript) then the compiler should resolve indices that count the stages it does have, because the chain iterator is designed to simply iterate on an ordered list or dictionary or whatever sequentially. right? after the distinct chains have been compiled, each one should be internally consistent, and then its colonel's job to make sure that the chain iterator gets clean plans with internally consistent indices. and i guess we don't need to rely on indices but the point is that the plans are still fully resolved and valid by the time they start executing on a given ingestion item. its invocation of the chain iteration remains dumb and gets a valid plan to execute. furthermore, colonel doesn't need to retain redundant copies of plans for each file in the ingest set, it only needs to hold the distinct plans and to be able to match them to ingest items programmatically, so that for each plan handed to chain executor, the right plan is executed for the given typed ingest item

Right on all three counts, and this lets me state the index invariant more precisely than I did last turn — your framing is the correct one.

**Indices are variant-local cursors; keys are identity.** Within a compiled chain, positional iteration is exactly what chain-executor does today — it walks `$Plan.Steps` sequentially, and the splice happens before it ever sees the list, so each variant is dense: TypeScript's chain is steps 0..N-2, PowerShell's 0..N-1, both internally complete with nothing missing *from their own perspective*. What died is only cross-variant index meaning — "step 2" names different processors in different chains, so any correlation across chains or across runs uses processor key or stage token. Index = loop counter, key = identity. The iterator never knows holes existed.

Worth naming the alternative this rejects, because it's the tempting wrong version: keeping template positions and marking unresolved holes as null/no-op steps in the list. That would preserve uniform indices across variants — and it would force the executor to know what a hole is, check for it at the top of the loop, and skip it. That's the executor learning about routing, which is precisely the leak the whole design exists to prevent. Splice, don't tombstone. Dense lists are what keep the executor dumb.

**On dedup, the existing code already shows the shape — and it's better than "one copy per worker."** Today `$planStepsForWorker` is built once at [colonel.v2:531](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:531) and the *same object* is passed to every worker at [colonel.v2:547](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:547) — in-process runspaces share the heap, so arguments pass by reference, not serialization. The family inherits this directly: colonel builds one dictionary of variantKey → step list (with the same deliberate minimal-shape copying that :531 does now), and all workers share that single object. Total plan storage for the entire run is V chains — not V per worker, and certainly not N per file. The per-item cost is one variant key in a parallel array riding alongside `$sliceItems`/`$sliceIdxs`. The one obligation this creates: the shared family must be treated as immutable by workers, which is already the implicit contract for today's shared steps array — worth making it an explicit line in the doc since the family is now a structure workers index into rather than a flat list they only read forward.

So the division of labor closes exactly as you stated it: compiler produces V internally-consistent frozen chains plus the ext→key match; colonel resolves the match per item at slice time and hands the executor a valid plan reference per invocation; executor iterates whatever ordered list it's given. Each layer's ignorance is intact — the executor doesn't know about routing, the colonel doesn't know about extensions' meanings (it just applies the map the compiler gave it), and only the compiler ever sees the routing tables.

# ok so lets look back and review the synthesis plan for this enhancement, all of the architectural pieces and the parsimonious implementation plan

Here's the full synthesis — the settled architecture, its invariants, and the implementation sequence in dependency order.

## The design in one paragraph

A declared **spine** fixes the canonical stage sequence; routed stages carry a meta-token (`$strip`, later others) pointing at a routing table. **Compile-Plan** instantiates the spine once per file class present in the corpus, resolving tokens per class and splicing unresolved ones out, producing a **plan family**: a small set of dense, frozen, internally-consistent chains plus an extension→variant map, all sharing one superset ISS. The **colonel** slices flat round-robin as today, resolves each item's variant key at slice time, and shares the single family object across workers. The **chain executor** receives a valid plan reference per item and iterates it — zero diff. Language-specificity exists only as data in the routing tables, consumed only by the compiler.

## Architectural pieces

**1. Routing artifact** — `processors/routing.json`, holding both the spine and the routing tables (token ids must agree across them, so one file). Stages in canonical order; fixed stages name a processor, routed stages name a token and table. Routes keyed by language id, extensions dot-less in the file and normalized on load, `Processors` entries are processor keys in declared order. Malformed file is a hard error.

**2. Compiler (Compile-Plan extension)** — emits `{Variants, Routing, Iss, ProcessorKeys}`. Variant identity is the tuple of token resolutions; only corpus-present tuples get compiled and bound, but every route in the file is validated against the processor manifest unconditionally. The default variant is the spine minus all holes and always exists. ISS registers the union of processors across variants — the pool layer already works this way.

**3. Colonel (Invoke-Plan)** — a third parallel array beside `$sliceItems`/`$sliceIdxs` carrying per-item variant keys, resolved from the Plan's own routing map. Family marshalled once with the existing minimal-shape discipline and shared by reference across all workers, as `$planStepsForWorker` is today. Worker prebuilds its `$plans` table once, then per item does a dictionary lookup.

**4. Executor and helpers** — untouched. Also untouched: budget resolution, bin-packing, stream harvesting, the index-stable output envelope, bag-helpers.

**5. Caller surface** — callers enable stages, never order them; `-StripComments` enables `$strip`. `-Processors` becomes a set under the canon, with a separately-named verbatim flag preserving deliberate-violation mode (not `-OrderStrict`; taken). The three soft cautions become compiler guarantees on the canon path and remain as warnings only under verbatim.

**6. Echo** — per-variant, with tokens as the unit of report: `$strip → rs-psstrip` vs `$strip → ∅` vs stage-absent are three distinguishable stories (routed-and-resolved / enabled-but-unresolved / disabled).

## Invariants (the doc's contract section)

- No component below plan compilation branches on file type.
- Stages self-establish their preconditions — the correctness layer that makes every variant, including spliced ones, correct by construction. The canon orders the nominal path only. This discipline must not erode.
- Variants are dense lists — splice, never tombstone. Indices are variant-local cursors; cross-variant identity is by processor key or token, never position.
- The shared family is immutable in workers.
- Tokens exist only as data read from the routing file, never as PowerShell string literals.
- Each stage's postcondition states which byte accounting survives it (the line-ending / SpanBytes interaction).

## Implementation sequence

The one open decision — where stage classification lives for out-of-spine processors like `tp-perplexity` — gates **only step 4**. Steps 1–3 are independent of it, so implementation can start now.

1. **Routing artifact + loader.** `routing.json` with the spine and a `Strip` table carrying the two existing routes (powershell, csharp). Loader does normalization and hard-error validation. Tests: malformed-file errors, extension normalization, route keys exist in manifest.
2. **Compiler.** Family emission in Compile-Plan. This is a pure function, so the test surface is the richest and cheapest: mixed extension set → expected variant tuples; dense chains of differing lengths; default variant present; spine invariants (measure last, strip precedes whitespace); corpus pruning vs validate-all; union ISS keys.
3. **Colonel.** The third slice array, family marshal, worker lookup — roughly a fifteen-line diff against [colonel.v2](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1). Tests: index-stable envelope over a mixed corpus; each item demonstrably ran its assigned chain (observable through bag provenance keys).
4. **Caller surface** *(after the classification decision)*: `-StripComments` → `$strip`, set-semantics for `-Processors`, the verbatim flag, caution retirement. End-to-end test: heterogeneous `.ps1`/`.cs`/`.md` ingest.
5. **Echo.** Per-variant ConfigEcho with token resolutions, asserted in the e2e test.

Each step lands with its tests through `tests/run-all.ps1` before the next begins, and steps 1–2 change no runtime behavior at all — existing monolithic calls keep working until step 3 wires the family through, which keeps every commit shippable on main.

If this matches your read of where we've landed, I'd capture it as `design/routing-and-templates.md` (target-shape doc, per house convention) with the classification question marked open, and then start on step 1.

---

1	# Pipeline Stage Architecture

3	RepoSnapshot V3 structures repository ingestion and snapshot artifact creation as a pipeline of isolated stages.

7	    Root[Root Path] --> Crawler[rs.core.crawler]
8	    Crawler -->|FsGraph + Rollups| Membrane[rs.core.membrane]
9	    Membrane -->|FilteredFsGraph| Ingest[rs.core.ingest]
10	    Ingest -->|Plan + Descriptors| Colonel[rs.core.colonel.v2]
11	    Colonel -->|DispatchOutput| Assemble[rs.core.assemble]
12	    Assemble -->|IR| Layout[rs.core.container Resolve-Layout]
13	    Layout -->|Layout| Shards[rs.core.shards New-ShardPlan]
14	    Shards -->|ShardPlan| Serialize[rs.core.serialize]
15	    Serialize -->|Receipts| Manifest[rs.core.manifest]
16	    Manifest -->|Tree Manifest + Shard Files| Output[Snapshot Artifacts]
17	```
18	
19	## Stage Responsibilities & Ownership
20	
21	| Stage | Module | Primary Role | Invariant / Boundary |
22	|---|---|---|---|
23	| **Crawl** | `rs.core.crawler.psm1` | Greedy BFS tree discovery | Stamps all facts free at filesystem vantage. Zero content reads. |
24	| **Membrane** | `rs.core.membrane.psm1` | Ingestion eligibility | Applies size/extension guards and 5-stage glob compilation (`Ignore` / `Selection`). |
25	| **Ingest** | `rs.core.ingest.psm1` | Ingest orchestration | Bridges Membrane to Colonel; translates profile configurations to compiled plans. |
26	| **Colonel** | `rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1` | Runspace dispatch | Compiles AST-validated processor scripts into ISS and dispatches items across worker pool. |
27	| **Assemble** | `rs.core.assemble.psm1` | In-memory collation | Collates dispatch results into IR; applies open element model and entry routing. |
28	| **Container** | `rs.core.container.psm1` | Wire formatting | Interprets `container.spec.jsonc`; measures and renders single-line `psr` rows. |
29	| **Shards** | `rs.core.shards.psm1` | Bin packing | Groups and packs entries into deterministic, bounded shards (`FrontLoad` or `Even`). |
30	| **Serialize** | `rs.core.serialize.psm1` | Disk emission | Writes shard files; enforces `Plan == File` size gate and records cursor receipts. |
31	| **Manifest** | `rs.core.manifest.psm1` | Manifest TOC emission | Renders Handlebars-lite TOC template with declarations and directory tree. |
32	
33	## Core Principles
34	
35	1. **Config Selects, Implementation Owns Sequence**:
36	   Callers configure which operations, stages, or columns are active; stage implementations own the execution sequence.
37	2. **Measurement at Point of Authority**:
38	   Facts are measured once by the component authoritative for that fact (e.g., offsets measured by serializer cursor, byte spans by container layout, file sizes by crawler).
39	3. **Open Element Model**:
40	   Payload elements attached by processor chains flow through assembly without requiring stage-specific schema modifications.
41
```

1	# Mutator Contracts & Shared Helpers
2	
3	Processors in `reposnapshot-v3/processors/` follow the harmonized content-mutator contract.
4	
5	## Contract Rules
6	
7	1. **Track-Agnostic Content Resolution**:
8	   - Reads `Content` (descriptor contract) else `Text` (tp-era).
9	   - Writes the modified string back to the exact key that was read.
10	   - If neither key is present on an item, the item is returned untouched without fabricating an empty string.
11	2. **Copy-on-Mutate**:
12	   - Clones input property bags into a new `PSCustomObject` via `Copy-Bag`.
13	   - Caller references and existing identity fields (`AbsolutePath`, `RelativePath`, `NodePath`, etc.) are preserved intact.
14	   - Preserves property insertion order using ordered dictionaries.
15	3. **Processing Trail**:
16	   - Each mutator appends an entry to the `Processing` array (`@{ Processor; Operations; ... }`).
17	   - Multiple passes through a mutator append records sequentially.
18	4. **Early Termination (`_ChainHalt`)**:
19	   - Step processors signal pipeline halt by attaching `_ChainHalt = $true`.
20	   - `chain-executor.ps1` detects the halt property, aborts remaining steps for that item, and returns the halted object for diagnostic routing.
21
```

Let me check the existing design directory conventions first so the doc matches house style.
Design is scribed. Committing just the two doc files (leaving your in-flight `rs.core.user.ps1`/`user-config.json` changes unstaged):
Design doc landed and committed (`2083d64`).

**[docs/spine-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/spine-and-routing.md)** captures the full synthesis in house doctrine style:

- **Two-layer contract** up front — precondition self-sufficiency as the correctness layer (explicitly cross-referenced to mutator-contracts, with the "this discipline is load-bearing for routing" warning), postcondition-derivation as the canon layer.
- **The routing artifact** — one `processors/routing.json` with spine + tables, a loader-rules table (hard error, normalization, key validation, token discipline including the `"$strip"` interpolation hazard).
- **Compilation** — plan family shape, variant-tuple identity, the always-present default variant with your TypeScript scenario as the worked example, splice-not-tombstone with the null-step alternative explicitly rejected, validate-all/bind-present split.
- **Dispatch** — flat round-robin with the third parallel array, superset ISS, shared-by-reference immutable family (V chains + N keys, never N chains), the untouched-components list, and the content-routing foreclosure with its known-but-unplanned escape hatch.
- **Caller surface** — enable-not-order, the `-Processors` set/verbatim fork, and the single **OPEN** decision (stage classification for out-of-spine processors like `tp-perplexity`) marked inline with the note that it gates only step 4.
- **Echo** — the three distinguishable stories as a table (`$strip → rs-psstrip` / `$strip → ∅` / stage absent).
- **Eight invariants** and the **five-step implementation sequence** with per-step test gates, noting steps 1–2 are behavior-neutral so every commit stays shippable on main.

Also added the ToC entry in [docs/README.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/README.md), tagged *(Target shape)* to distinguish it from the as-built docs. I went with `spine-and-routing.md` rather than the earlier `routing-and-templates.md` offer since "templates with holes" was vocabulary the spine design superseded.

Ready to start step 1 (routing artifact + loader) whenever you want — or settle the classification question first if you'd rather close the open decision while the context is warm.

[main f01c4c8] docs: settle stage classification — spine is the single processor registry
 1 file changed, 21 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)
```

The settlement I'd argue for — and it's stronger than a preference, because one of the three candidates turns out to dominate once you notice the strippers are already classified:

**The spine is the single processor registry. Classification is structural: a processor is classified by where it appears in `routing.json`, and every processor appears in exactly one stage.**

The dominance argument: routed processors need their route entries *regardless* of which option wins — `rs-psstrip` must appear under `Strip` for routing to work at all. So route membership already classifies every language stripper for free; a `Stage` field in `configs/<key>.json` would duplicate for strippers what routing.json already states, and add a declaration surface only for the handful of fixed-stage processors. Enumerating those directly in the spine costs one line each and buys: single authority, hard-error posture where classification needs it (`configs/` keeps its warn-and-default semantics and stays pure config — no reserved keys), and the same referential-integrity argument that fused spine and routing tables into one file — a stage rename can't orphan classifications that live in the same document. It's also just the routing instance of the codebase's existing Core Principle #1 in [stage-architecture.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/stage-architecture.md): config selects, implementation owns sequence.

The consequences that fall out, each a simplification:

- **`tp-perplexity` gets a spine entry** — `{ "Stage": "parse", "Processor": "tp-perplexity" }`, after read, before `$strip`. Declared-but-not-enabled: it runs only when a caller enables it, so its presence in the spine changes no nominal chain. When content-type routing is wanted later, the entry flips to a `$parse` token with a `Parse` table — additive, as the doc already anticipated.
- **Canon set-mode semantics close cleanly**: naming a processor enables its stage. For routed stages that enables the *token* — routing still decides per file class, and `-StripComments` becomes literally "enable `$strip`," so the toggle and the set spelling converge on one mechanism.
- **Exactly-once upgrades the old collision caution to a validation error.** One fixed stage, or route membership under one token — with multiple routes under the *same* token free to share a processor (a future generic stripper serving two languages is legitimate and still one stage).
- **Error-on-use, test-for-completeness.** Naming an unregistered processor under canon mode is a hard error naming both fixes (register it, or run verbatim). But an unreferenced *file* in `processors/` is inert — dropping a WIP processor in never breaks a run. Completeness for the shipped set (manifest ⊆ spine) is a repo test, per the tests-are-the-only-enforcement doctrine, not a runtime check.

Option C survives as exactly what it should be — the backstop for the unregistered case — rather than a mode anyone is pushed into. Updating the doc now.
Now the invariants list and implementation sequence:
Classification is settled and committed (`f01c4c8`). The doc's OPEN block is replaced with a **Classification** subsection under Caller Surface, and the changes ripple through:

- **Spine example** now shows `tp-perplexity` registered as a fixed `parse` stage between read and `$strip` — declared-but-not-enabled, so no nominal chain changes.
- **Loader rules** gained the exactly-once row: one fixed stage or route membership under one token, with same-token route sharing explicitly legal (a future generic stripper serving two languages stays one stage). Double appearance is now a hard validation error, superseding the old duplicate-splice caution.
- **Set-mode semantics** are pinned: naming a processor enables its stage; for routed stages that enables the token and routing still decides per file, which makes `-StripComments` and naming a `Strip` member the same operation spelled two ways.
- **Failure postures** are separated by design: unregistered processor *named* under canon → hard error with both fixes stated; unreferenced processor *file* on disk → inert, with shipped-set completeness (manifest ⊆ spine) enforced by a repo test rather than at runtime.
- **Invariant 9** added (classification is structural; `configs/` carries no ordering metadata), and **step 4 is ungated** — the implementation sequence now has no blocking decisions.

The only remaining TBD in the whole design is cosmetic: the verbatim flag's name. Everything else is closed — ready to start step 1 (routing.json + loader) whenever you say.

```
[main 70a0b14] docs: registry.json keyed on processors — normalized Spine/Languages/Processors schema
 2 files changed, 35 insertions(+), 30 deletions(-)
```

That keying is an upgrade, and it buys two things the stage-keyed draft had to legislate: with one entry per processor key, **exactly-once becomes true by construction** (a JSON object can't hold two entries for `rs-psstrip`, and one `Stage` field can't name two stages), and **completeness collapses to a set comparison** — registry keys against the manifest, the same keyspace as `configs/<key>.json`, so all the one-file-per-key mechanical checks line up on one axis.

One normalization is needed to make processor-keying sound, though: two of the facts in play aren't per-processor facts. Canonical *order* is a spine fact — hanging it on dictionary key order would make alphabetizing the file a pipeline change. And *extensions* are language facts — if `rs.ps.strip` and a future `rs.ps.dedoc` each carried their own extension lists, they could disagree about what "powershell" means. So the registry gets three sections, each fact declared once: `Spine` (ordered stage ids), `Languages` (language → extensions), `Processors` (keyed on processor: stage, plus language when routed). Routes are then **derived**, not declared — the `$strip` route for powershell is "the processors whose entries say so, in registry order" — which incidentally fixes a latent incoherence in my earlier draft, where per-route extension lists could have made the same language mean different file sets under different tokens. Updating the doc:
Now the compilation, classification, and invariant sections that referenced route tables:
Committed (`70a0b14`). The artifact is now `processors/registry.json` with the normalized three-section schema, and the doc reflects the structural wins your keying produces:

- **`Processors` keyed on processor keys** — same keyspace as the manifest and `configs/`, so exactly-once is enforced by the data structure (one entry, one `Stage`) rather than by a validation rule, and the completeness test collapses to `manifest keys = registry keys`. The old exactly-once loader rule is gone; it's now impossible to violate.
- **`Spine` and `Languages` stay as small tables** because those two facts aren't per-processor: canonical order can't ride on dictionary key order without making an alphabetize a pipeline change, and extensions declared per-stripper could disagree about what a language means once multi-processor routes exist. Each fact declared once: order on the spine, extensions on the language, classification on the processor.
- **Routes are derived, not declared** — `$strip`/powershell members are the processors whose entries say so, in registry declaration order. No route table exists to fall out of sync with entries.
- **Ripple effects**: file class is now cleanly *language* (via `Languages`, unmapped → default), which makes the variant-tuple story crisper than the per-route extension lists allowed; the loader rules table gained stage/language cross-validation and one-language-per-extension (routing must be a function); `Language` accepts string-or-array so a future generic stripper can serve several languages while remaining one stage.

The design is fully closed now — the verbatim flag's name remains the only TBD, and nothing gates on it. Step 1 is scribing `registry.json` itself plus the loader, whenever you're ready.

# well there's already established convention for ordering. there is prescribed rank-ordering of different stages -- file_read has to happen before anything else, strippers for any language would occupy the same spot in an otherwise equivalent chain, whitespace and indentation processors happen in some order relative to eachother, and all mutating processors such as content_meta happens after any mutations because its point is to gather metadata on the content that will be rendered downstream, not the pre-preprocessed raw data. So there's an implicit canonical ordering of processor categories or families, and certain categories include language-specific routing while others do not. i think i'm actually going to pencil out tp-perplexity since that's an oddball from the document ingestion lineage that wont likely run until i revisit the subject after finishing code ingestion e2e and the general architecture that will be shared between code vs non-code ingestion. and of course all of this is an abstraction behind what ultimatley boils down to flat lists of processors in a chain

Penciling out tp-perplexity actually lands it in a slot the design already built: it becomes the live instance of the "unreferenced file on disk → inert" posture rather than the example fixed-stage entry. The one consequence to handle deliberately is the completeness test — `manifest ⊆ registry` would now fail on it, so the test carries a documented deferral rather than the registry carrying a parked entry. And verbatim mode can still run it, so the document-lineage experiments stay reachable without registration. Updating the doc, including your family/rank-order framing and the sharper measure-last rationale:
Committed (`b26ec48`). Three adjustments:

- **The registry example is now the pure code-ingestion set** — spine `read → $strip → indent → whitespace → measure`, six processors, no `parse` stage. A new sentence under the schema states your framing directly: spine ids name **processor families** in prescribed rank order, a family is either fixed (one member) or language-routed (a token), and rank belongs to the family — which is why strippers for any language occupy the same slot in otherwise equivalent chains.
- **tp-perplexity is deferred, not deleted**: it's now the doc's live instance of the unregistered-file posture — inert on disk, canon mode errors if named, verbatim still runs it for document-lineage experiments, and the completeness test carries it as a documented deferral so `manifest ⊆ registry` stays green until you revisit the code/non-code shared architecture.
- **The measure-last derivation got your sharper rationale**: measurement describes the content rendered downstream, not the raw input — that's *why* every mutation invalidates it, rather than just the rule stated flatly.

And yes — your closing point is the design's whole contract in one line, and the doc holds it at both ends: the registry, spine, tokens, and families exist only at compile time, and what crosses into the executor is exactly what crosses today — flat lists of `@{Key; Fn; Config}`, one per variant, dumbly iterated. The abstraction buys the resolution; the runtime never sees it.

Since manifest discovery is a non-recursive glob over `processors/*.ps1`, the move takes `tp-perplexity` out of the manifest automatically, which has two doc consequences: the completeness test no longer needs a deferral exclusion (manifest ⊆ registry holds plainly), and the "verbatim can still run it" claim is now false — verbatim validates against the manifest too. The doc now records deferral as structural: `processors/deferred/` sits outside the non-recursive manifest glob, making a parked processor invisible to manifest, registry, and the completeness test alike — no documented-exclusion mechanism needed, and the step-2 gate is back to plain `manifest ⊆ registry`. Restoring the file plus adding a registry entry is the whole re-activation gesture. I also dropped the stale "verbatim can still run it" claim, since verbatim validates against the manifest too and can no longer see the parked file.

One observation from checking for orphaned configs, for when step 1 lands: `configs/` already holds pre-staged config files for eleven future strippers in the dotted scheme (`rs.py.strip`, `rs.ts.strip`, `rs.nu.strip`, `rs.lean.strip`, `rs.jl.strip`, `rs.mat.strip`, `rs.r.strip`, `rs.c.strip`, `rs.sh.strip`, `rs.rs.strip`, `rs.js.strip`) — a ready-made roster for the registry's `Languages` and `Processors` sections. It also tees up one small decision for that step: whether `rs-psstrip`/`rs-csstrip` migrate to the dotted scheme (`rs.ps.strip`/`rs.cs.strip`) at the same time, since the registry is the natural moment to normalize the keyspace in one motion.

105	### Classification
106	
107	The registry is keyed on processor keys, so classification *is* the entry:
108	
109	- Every processor has exactly one entry declaring its `Stage` — with `Language` qualification when that stage is a token. Declared is not enabled: an entry fixes position only; the stage runs when a caller enables it.
110	- **Routes are derived** — a language stripper is a `$strip`/`powershell` processor because its entry says so; the compiler assembles routes from entries, and no separate route table exists to fall out of sync.
111	- `configs/<key>.json` carries **no ordering metadata** — it stays pure config with its warn-and-default posture; classification lives in the registry, where hard-error validation and referential integrity already are (a stage or language rename cannot orphan classifications living in the same document).
112	
113	Consequences:
114	
115	- **Set-mode resolution**: naming a processor key enables its stage. For a routed stage this enables the token — routing still decides per file class, so `-Processors 'rs.ps.strip'` on a mixed corpus enables `$strip` wherever it resolves. `-StripComments` and naming a `Strip` member are the same operation spelled two ways.
116	- **Unregistered processor named under canon mode** → hard error stating both fixes: register it in the spine, or run verbatim.
117	- **Unreferenced processor *file* on disk** → inert, not an error — a WIP processor can land in `processors/` without breaking runs. Completeness for the shipped set (manifest ⊆ registry keys) is a repo test, not a runtime check.
118	- **Deferral is structural**: `processors/deferred/` sits outside the non-recursive manifest glob, so a parked processor (`tp-perplexity`, document-ingestion lineage) is invisible to manifest, registry, and completeness alike. Restoring the file to `processors/` is the re-activation gesture — plus a registry entry to run under canon.
119	
120	## Echo
121	
122	Tokens are the unit of report. Per-variant ConfigEcho must keep three stories distinguishable:
123	
124	| Story | Echo |
125	|---|---|
126	| Routed and resolved | `$strip → rs.ps.strip` |
127	| Enabled but unresolved for this class | `$strip → ∅` (stripping was *on*; no resolver existed — e.g. TypeScript comments present for this reason, not because stripping was off) |
128	| Stage disabled for the run | stage absent from every variant |
129	
130	Any manifest structure that tabulates per-step data across files keys by variant + processor key, never by position.
131	
132	## Invariants
133	
134	1. No component below plan compilation branches on file type. The compiler alone reads the registry; the colonel applies an opaque map; the executor iterates what it is given.
135	2. Stages self-establish preconditions (correctness layer); the spine orders the nominal path (quality layer). Neither substitutes for the other.
136	3. Variants are dense — splice, never tombstone.
137	4. Indices are variant-local cursors; cross-variant and cross-run identity is by processor key or stage token, never position. Chains of unequal length coexist in one run.
138	5. The shared plan family is immutable in workers.
139	6. The default variant always exists and equals the spine minus all holes.
140	7. Tokens exist only as data, never as PowerShell string literals.

142	9. Every processor is classified by its single registry entry — one stage, with language qualification when routed. `configs/` carries no ordering metadata; classification is structural.

Let me ground this in the current state — the doc's later sections as they now stand, and what exists on disk.
## Where things stand

The design is fully settled and committed; **no implementation code exists yet**. `processors/registry.json` is the first unbuilt artifact — everything landed so far is either documentation or preparatory refactoring that the design provoked.

## What got settled this session

The through-line was pushing every decision out of runtime and into compilation, and it went further than expected: **chain-executor's diff is zero**. The pieces, in the order they closed:

**Plan families.** `Compile-Plan` stops emitting one chain and emits N dense chains — one per file class present in the corpus — plus an extension→variant map. Language-specificity is data read only by the compiler; the colonel applies an opaque map, the executor iterates a flat list exactly as today.

**The two-layer contract**, which is the load-bearing insight and the one most at risk of erosion later: precondition self-sufficiency is the *correctness* layer (every stage combination valid by construction, which is why spliced variants just work), and the spine canon is the *quality* layer (nominal ordering, derived by "A follows B if B can violate A's postcondition"). The redundancy across processors is deliberate, not debt.

**The registry**, keyed on processor keys with three normalized sections — `Spine` (family rank order), `Languages` (extensions), `Processors` (classification). Your inversion to processor-keying made exactly-once true by construction and collapsed the completeness check to a set comparison; routes became derived rather than declared, which eliminated a whole class of desync.

**Splice, don't tombstone.** Unresolved tokens are removed, not nulled — so variants have different lengths, indices are variant-local cursors, and cross-variant identity is by key or token. The alternative would have forced the executor to learn what a hole is.

**Dispatch stays flat.** Round-robin over the item list with a third parallel array carrying variant keys, one superset ISS (the pool layer already works this way), one shared immutable family object — V chains plus N keys, never N chains.

## What's in place

| Committed | |
|---|---|
| `2083d64`→`b26ec48` | Design doc + README index entry |
| `b10e884`, `4155977` | `tp-perplexity` parked in `processors/deferred/`, outside the manifest glob |
| `0d085d0` | Strippers renamed to `rs.ps.strip` / `rs.cs.strip` (8 file renames + references), battery green at 23 suites / 1417 asserts |

Plus a useful accident of prior work: `configs/` already holds eleven pre-staged stripper configs in the dotted scheme (`rs.py.strip`, `rs.ts.strip`, `rs.nu.strip`, `rs.lean.strip`, and so on), so the keyspace the registry will be born into is already normalized and the roster for future `Languages` entries is sitting there.

## The plan from here

**Step 1 — registry artifact and loader.** Write `registry.json` with the current six processors, plus a loader doing extension normalization and hard-error validation (stage/language cross-references, one-language-per-extension, keys exist in manifest). Behavior-neutral; nothing consumes it yet.

**Step 2 — the compiler.** Family emission in `Compile-Plan`. This is the richest test surface in the whole plan and the cheapest to exercise, because compilation is a pure function — mixed extension sets in, expected variant tuples out, no runspaces or filesystem involved. Still behavior-neutral.

**Step 3 — the colonel.** The third slice array, family marshal, worker lookup table. Roughly a fifteen-line diff against [rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1); this is where the family first actually runs.

**Step 4 — caller surface.** `-StripComments` → `$strip`, `-Processors` set semantics, the verbatim flag, caution retirement.

**Step 5 — per-variant echo** with token resolutions.

Steps 1 and 2 change no runtime behavior, so every commit stays shippable on main and the monolithic path keeps working until step 3 wires it through.

## Two things to carry into step 1

The only genuine TBD left is cosmetic: **the verbatim flag's name** (`-OrderStrict` is taken by bin packing). Nothing gates on it.

And one detail worth having a test for at step 2: since variant identity is the *tuple of token resolutions*, a language declared in `Languages` with no `$strip` member resolves the same tuple as `default` and should collapse into the same compiled chain rather than producing a duplicate. That matters immediately if you populate `Languages` from the eleven pre-staged configs before their processors exist — which is otherwise a reasonable thing to do, since those entries would be inert until a processor claims them.

Yes — and your first clause settles a case I'd left dangling, so both halves are now closed. Two different collapses are in play, one level apart:

**Step-level collapse (yours).** Exactly the settled splice semantics. Your `[strip, whitespace, indent]` example resolves to `[strip, indent, whitespace]` for PowerShell items and `[indent, whitespace]` for the unrouted ones in the same run — one step shorter, everything else in the same relative order. (The indent/whitespace ordering comes from the spine rather than how you typed it, since set mode means position is the compiler's — but that's orthogonal to the collapse you're describing, which is right as stated.)

**Variant-level collapse (what I was pointing at).** One level up: two *classes* whose resolution tuples come out identical should share a single compiled chain object rather than producing two byte-identical chains keyed by class name.

Your "don't declare a member that doesn't exist" makes the phantom case impossible by construction — under processor-keying there's no way to assert "python has a stripper" except by an `rs.py.strip` entry the loader validates against the manifest, so `Languages` only ever lists claimed languages, unclaimed extensions are simply unmapped, and they fall to `default` without a redundant python-shaped class ever existing.

Where variant dedup still earns its keep is the reverse case the doc already permits: `Language` as an array, one generic stripper claiming several languages. A `rs.c.strip` serving c/cpp/objc gives three classes one resolution — one compiled chain, one variant key, three extensions mapping into it. And it generalizes once a second token lands, where classes can agree on one token and differ on another.

Scribing both.
Committed (`efdd354`). Two lines, both closing loose ends:

- **Loader rule — no phantom members**: `Languages` lists only languages some processor claims; membership can't be declared for a processor that doesn't exist, so unclaimed extensions stay unmapped and fall to `default`. That kills the case I flagged at the end of the last summary — populating `Languages` from the eleven pre-staged configs ahead of their processors is now explicitly not the move.
- **Variant identity keys on the resolution tuple, not the class name**, with the c/cpp/objc shared-stripper case as the concrete instance.

The step-2 test gate already covers this ("mixed extension set → expected variant tuples"); the dedup case is now a named instance of it rather than something to rediscover.

Nothing else moved. Step 1 — writing `registry.json` and its loader — is still the next unbuilt thing, and it's now fully specified: six processors, two languages, five spine ids, seven loader rules.

Let me ground this in what the flag would actually be preserving.
It's the least-motivated piece of the design, and your confusion is warranted — let me lay out what it actually is, then argue it may not need to exist.

**It's not a new feature; it's today's behavior kept alive.** Right now `-Processors` is a literal ordered sequence: whatever array you pass becomes the chain, in that order, for every file. Your own [user-config.json](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/user-config.json) uses it that way. The canon design takes that spelling away — under set semantics the compiler orders things and your array position stops meaning anything. So the fork is: does `-Processors` stay a literal sequence, or become a set? Both can't own the same parameter. "Verbatim" was my proposal to keep the old meaning reachable under a different flag rather than deleting it.

**The part that's probably confusing you is what verbatim does about routing.** If you hand over a literal chain containing `rs.ps.strip` and your corpus has `.cs` files in it, the PowerShell stripper runs on the C# files — because verbatim means no family is compiled at all. One chain, every file, exactly the current code path. That's incoherent as a production mode, and deliberately so: it's a diagnostic instrument where you've asked for precisely what you typed. Which is also why it can't be a subtle behavior of `-Processors` — it has to be a flag you consciously reach for.

That also explains the "cautions only" phrase. Those three advisories at [rs.core.user.ps1:251-258](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:251) — chain omits whitespace, content_meta isn't last, Columns wants content_meta but no step runs — currently print on every run. Under canon they become impossible, since the compiler places measure last by construction, so they'd stop printing entirely. Under verbatim they're exactly the right warnings and stay.

**Now the honest part.** The use cases I invoked for it are mostly *test* use cases: probing whether the canon's postcondition claims hold, running a processor twice, measuring content before and after mutation. But those don't need a CLI flag — `Compile-Plan` takes an ordered `-Steps` array and is directly callable, which is exactly what [colonel-validation.tests.ps1](utils/reposnapshot/tests/colonel-validation.tests.ps1) already does. The instrument for deliberately violating invariants exists at the module layer today and isn't going anywhere.

So the flag only earns its place if you want deviant chains to produce **full snapshot artifacts** — crawl through serialize, real files on disk, to compare outputs under different orderings. If your diagnostics stop at "what does this chain do to the bags," the module API covers it and the flag is dead weight.

Worth noting your live config is already canon-conformant — `rs.ps.strip → rs-indent → rs-whitespace` is spine order — so switching `-Processors` to a pure set changes nothing about how you actually run the tool.

My recommendation: **drop it from the plan.** Make `-Processors` a set, delete the fork, and if you later want deviant full-pipeline runs, add the flag then with a real use case in hand rather than a predicted one. That removes the last TBD (the naming problem disappears with it) and makes step 4 smaller. Say the word and I'll strike it from the doc.

Confirmed precisely — `rs-whitespace` runs `if ('op' -in $ops)` blocks in fixed source order, so the `Operations` array is pure membership and the sequence belongs to the code. Same shape one level up. Scribing that as the precedent, with the flag named.

**`-RunVerbatim` is named**, with the routing semantics I explained now written down: literal chain, no family, no routing, every file — so a language-specific processor in a verbatim chain runs on everything regardless of class, which is precisely what was asked for.

**One asymmetry documented while it was visible**: unregistered processor under canon is a hard error, but `rs-whitespace` records an unknown op as a skip receipt with `Reason = 'UnknownOp'`. Someone would eventually notice that inconsistency, so the doc now states the justification — an unknown op has an obvious no-op fallback, an unplaceable processor doesn't, and placing it arbitrarily would be worse than refusing.

The step 4 test gate gained the verbatim case (out-of-canon chain runs and still prints its cautions), which is what keeps the two modes from quietly converging as the canon path grows guarantees.

The design now has no open items. Step 1 — `registry.json` plus loader — is next.

Right — the analogy carries the selection/sequence split, not the ordering mechanism. `rs-whitespace` has one flat source sequence; the compiler resolves two levels, and my doc sentence flattened that. Sharpening it:
Committed (`d804722`). The analogy is now explicitly scoped to the selection/sequence split, with the actual resolution stated as two-level:

- **Across families** — `Spine` ranks the families; a member inherits its family's rank.
- **Within a family** — registry declaration order among members.

One thing that fell out while writing it: within-family order binds only among members that *co-apply to the same file class*. Two strippers for different languages never occupy the same chain, so their relative position in the registry is moot — the within-family ordering question is live only for multi-member routes, like a language declaring both a stripper and a dedoc pass. Fixed families have one member and no within-family question at all.

That has a practical consequence for step 1: the registry can be written in whatever grouping reads best (all strippers together, or grouped by language) without the arrangement carrying unintended ordering meaning — the only arrangement that binds is the relative order of co-applying members.

You're right — "sorting or regrouping the registry" reads as though changing declared values shouldn't matter, which is backwards. The distinction I collapsed is **declared values vs. file layout**: `Spine`'s element order and `Rank` values are authoritative data (change them and chains change, as intended), while the `Processors` object's *key* order is incidental. Fixing the wording:
Committed (`f9f013b`). Invariant 10 now reads:

> Ordering is declared, never inferred from layout. The `Spine` array's element order and any `Rank` values are authoritative — edit them and compiled chains change, exactly as intended. The `Processors` object's *key* order is not data: alphabetizing or regrouping those entries changes no chain.

The whole point is the asymmetry between the two containers: `Spine` is an **array**, so its element order is declared data; `Processors` is an **object**, so its key order is an artifact of how the file happens to be written. Both live in the same file, which is exactly why the distinction needed stating.

The step 1 test gate now says the same thing as a pair of assertions — permuting `Processors` keys changes no compiled chain, permuting `Spine` does — so the two halves are checked in opposite directions rather than only the "nothing changed" half.

Read back: `Processors` is the spine with ordering split into explicit `Group` (across families) and `Rank` (within), and `Routing` nests route tables under the token itself.

Two things your format does better than what I'd written. Within-group rank now applies to **fixed** processors too — `rs-indent` and `rs-whitespace` as one group ranked 1/2, where my doc had them as separate single-member families and asserted "fixed families have exactly one member." Yours is the better generalization; one mechanism covers both cases. And nesting routes under `$strip` means a second token is a sibling key with no schema change, rather than a parallel top-level table that has to agree with the spine by convention.

**Two concrete errors in the file:**

1. `"processor": "rs.cs.strip.cs"` — no such file. It's `rs.cs.strip.ps1`.
2. `$strip` carries `"Language": "powershell"` in `Processors` while `Routing.$strip` lists both powershell and csharp. That pins the token to one language, which contradicts the routing table's whole job. I read it as vestigial — should be `null`, since the token's language is decided per file by `Routing`.

**Three things the loader needs settled:**

- **Filenames vs keys.** `Routing` names files (`rs.ps.strip.ps1`); the manifest is keyed on basename. Recommend keys — the `.cs` typo is precisely the failure that manifest-validated keys catch at load, and it would keep `Routing`, `configs/<key>.json`, and `-Processors` on one keyspace.
- **`Group`/`Rank` as strings.** `"10"` sorts before `"2"` lexicographically. Fine at five groups, a trap later — recommend integers.
- **Rank base is inconsistent** (`file-read` and `$strip` at 0; indent/whitespace/content_meta at 1/2/1). Harmless if only relative order within a group binds, but worth picking one convention.

One consequence to note: completeness is no longer a single set comparison, since routed processors live in `Routing` rather than `Processors` — the check becomes manifest = (non-token `Processors` keys ∪ `Routing` processor refs).

And I'd take `Enabled` as a declared default that caller flags override (`-StripComments`, `-Processors`) rather than a hard on/off — confirm and I'll write it that way.

Say go and I'll apply those fixes to `sequencing.json` and realign the doc: rename references, replace the `Spine` array with the Group/Rank model, fold in the fixed-family-can-have-members correction, and rewrite the completeness rule.
