```
=== design doc ===
The shortest correct model is three passes: **enable**, **route**, **sort**. `Group`/`Rank` already *are* the spine, so you do not need a separate `Spine` array. You also do not get one chain for the run — `$strip` occupies one slot that fans out per file class.

Against this `user-config.json` (`IncludeProcessors` + `*.ps1`/`*.psm1`) that resolves to:

```
powershell: file-read → rs.ps.strip → rs-indent → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta
default: file-read → rs-indent → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta
```

Add `.cs` to the corpus and a `csharp` variant appears with `rs.cs.strip` in the same Group 2 slot. Those two strippers never co-exist on one file.

### 1. Enable (filter + Requires closure)

`IncludeProcessors` is a set, not an order. Union `Default: true` (`file-read`), then close `Requires`:

```powershell
$seq   = Get-Content -Raw .\processors\sequencing.json | ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable
$cfg   = Get-Content -Raw .\user-config.json          | ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable
$procs = $seq.Processors

$wanted = [System.Collections.Generic.HashSet[string]]::new([string[]]$cfg.IncludeProcessors)
foreach ($k in $procs.Keys) { if ($procs[$k].Default) { [void]$wanted.Add($k) } }
do {
    $n = $wanted.Count
    foreach ($k in @($wanted)) {
        foreach ($r in @($procs[$k].Requires)) { [void]$wanted.Add($r) }
    }
} while ($wanted.Count -gt $n)
```

That yields `{ file-read, $strip, rs-indent, rs-whitespace, rs-content_meta }`. Array position in the config is ignored.

### 2. Route (slice `$strip` against crawler `Extension`)

Fixed keys map to `processors\<key>.ps1`. `$strip` does not — there is no `$strip.ps1`. `Routing.$strip` names the real files, and crawler already stamped `Extension` (leading-dot, e.g. `.ps1`) on every descriptor. Use that; do not re-derive from the path.

```powershell
$dotless = [System.Collections.Generic.HashSet[string]]::new(
    [string[]]($eligible.Extension | ForEach-Object { $_.TrimStart('.').ToLowerInvariant() })
)

# languages actually present
$classes = [System.Collections.Generic.List[string]]::new()
[void]$classes.Add('default')
foreach ($token in $seq.Routing.Keys) {
    if (-not $wanted.Contains($token)) { continue }
    foreach ($lang in $seq.Routing[$token].GetEnumerator()) {
        $hit = $false
        foreach ($e in $lang.Value.extensions) {
            if ($dotless.Contains([string]$e)) { $hit = $true; break }
        }
        if ($hit) { [void]$classes.Add($lang.Key) }
    }
}
```

Per class, walk `$wanted` and **splice** an unresolved token rather than leaving a hole:

```powershell
function Resolve-Steps([string]$class) {
    foreach ($k in $wanted) {
        $meta = $procs[$k]
        $file = "$k.ps1"
        if ($seq.Routing.Contains($k)) {
            if ($class -eq 'default' -or -not $seq.Routing[$k].Contains($class)) { continue }
            $file = $seq.Routing[$k][$class].processor   # rs.ps.strip.ps1
        }
        [pscustomobject]@{
            Key   = [IO.Path]::GetFileNameWithoutExtension($file)
            File  = $file
            Group = [int]$meta.Group
            Rank  = [int]$meta.Rank
        }
    }
}
```

`required: true` on a route is a disk check when `$strip` is enabled (`processors\rs.ps.strip.ps1` must exist). It does not force that language into every chain. Bind only classes whose extensions actually appear.

### 3. Sort (canon)

```powershell
$variants = [ordered]@{}
foreach ($class in $classes) {
    $variants[$class] = @(Resolve-Steps $class | Sort-Object Group, Rank)
}
```

Cast `Group`/`Rank` to `[int]` — they are strings in the JSON, and string sort will eventually put `"10"` before `"2"`. `Sort-Object` is the right tool here: it is stable, so equal `(Group, Rank)` stays in input order, which you should not rely on. Two `$strip` members sharing Group 2 / Rank 0 is fine because routing makes them mutually exclusive.

ISS registration is the **union of `Key`s across variants**. Dispatch is a per-file lookup: `Extension` → class → frozen step list. That is the whole compiler.

### Things that will bite

- **`$strip` is data.** In PowerShell `"$strip"` interpolates to empty. Keep the token in JSON / hashtable keys / single-quoted strings.
- **`IncludeProcessors` is not wired yet.** `rs.core.user.ps1` still reads `Processors` as a literal ordered list of concrete keys (`rs.ps.strip`, not `$strip`) and hard-prepends `file-read`. The recipe above is the replacement for that block.
- **Do not flatten both strippers into one execution chain.** The union list is what you load; each file runs one dense variant.

```

Read all three. The recipe is sound — enable/route/sort is the right decomposition, and it's tighter than what I'd written: `Group`/`Rank` genuinely subsume the `Spine` array, and `Requires` gives closure without an ordering vocabulary. Let me verify one claim in it before assessing.
The model is right, and it's tighter than mine. `Group`/`Rank` genuinely subsume the `Spine` array — one mechanism ranks families and orders co-applying members, and rank-0-means-solo makes group cardinality declarative rather than a separate rule. `Requires` is the better call too: dependency closure without inventing an ordering vocabulary, since order stays with Group/Rank. And keeping keys as bare filenames avoids the extra naming layer with no loss, because `$strip` is the one key that *isn't* a file and `Routing` is exactly where that exception gets resolved.

One confirmation worth stating, since it's non-obvious: **config deferral still works for routed steps.** `Resolve-Steps` sets `Key` to the resolved basename (`rs.ps.strip`), and `Compile-Plan` defers bare-config steps to `configs/<Key>.json` — so a token picks up `configs/rs.ps.strip.json` automatically. No token-level config file is needed or possible, which is correct.

**Live right now:** `IncludeProcessors` is inert. [rs.core.user.ps1:175](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:175) looks up the key `'Processors'`, which the config no longer has, so `$Processors` stays null and the default branch builds `file-read → rs-whitespace → rs-content_meta`. Runs today silently drop `$strip` and `rs-indent`. Your doc says "not wired yet"; this is the exact line and the exact chain you're getting meanwhile.

**Three validations the loader should carry**, all cheap and all catching silent-wrong rather than loud-wrong:

- **Rank-0 exclusivity** — a group containing a Rank 0 member must contain only that member. It's your stated convention; nothing enforces it, and violating it makes the sort order depend on `$wanted` iteration order.
- **`Requires` must respect Group/Rank.** Closure and ordering are separate mechanisms here, so an edge X→Y where Y sorts *after* X would enable Y and then run it too late, silently. Worth asserting every edge points backward at load.
- **Route processor files must exist on disk** when their token is enabled — this is the `required: true` behavior your doc describes, but no `required` field is actually present in `sequencing.json`. Either add it or make existence unconditional.

**Two smaller things:** `default` is added to `$classes` unconditionally, so an all-`.ps1` corpus compiles an unused default variant — prune it when every present extension is claimed. And `Group`/`Rank` are strings that your recipe casts to `[int]` at read; simpler to store them as ints and skip the cast, since nothing else wants them as text.

Also stale: [user-config.json](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/user-config.json)'s `$comment` still says "Processors defaults defer to processors/configs/<Key>.json" — the key is now `IncludeProcessors`, and the deferral sentence is worth restating as "bare keys defer to `configs/<key>.json`; `$strip` defers to its resolved processor's config."

**Reconciliation question.** [docs/spine-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/spine-and-routing.md) is now substantially superseded — it describes a `Spine` array, a `Languages` section, `processors.registry.json`, and asserts fixed families have exactly one member, all of which `sequencing.json` supersedes. My recommendation: rewrite that doc to the Group/Rank model as durable architecture doctrine (invariants, two-layer contract, dispatch, echo — the parts that survive unchanged), and let your `issues/` doc stay the implementation recipe. Alternatively I retire it entirely and fold the surviving invariants into yours.

Say which, and whether you want me to wire enable/route/sort into `rs.core.user.ps1` and `Compile-Plan` now.

**[result: toolu_01LfWn6PD7...]**
```
=== ingest ===
#Requires -Version 7.5

using namespace System.Collections.Generic

Import-Module "$PSScriptRoot/rs.core.internals.psm1" -Force

<#
.SYNOPSIS
    RepoSnapshot V3 ingest mediation — bridges membrane output to colonel dispatch.

.DESCRIPTION
    Extracts eligible file descriptors from the filtered filesystem graph, compiles
    the processing plan via Compile-Plan, and dispatches items across the worker pool
    via Invoke-Plan.

    See docs/stage-architecture.md and docs/colonel-and-iss.md for architecture details.
#>

#region Invoke-Ingest
function Invoke-Ingest
{
    <#
    .SYNOPSIS
        Compiles the processing plan and dispatches eligible items.

    .PARAMETER FilteredFsGraph
        Output from Invoke-Membrane containing Graph and Skipped entries.

    .OUTPUTS
        [PSCustomObject] @{ Results; Skipped; Errors; Warnings; Streams; Budget; Timing }
    #>
    [CmdletBinding()]
    param(
        [Parameter(Mandatory)]
        [object]$FilteredFsGraph
    )

    DynamicParam
    {
        $ingestOwn = @('FilteredFsGraph')

        $dict = New-ForwardedParamDictionary -TargetCommand 'Compile-Plan' `
            -ExcludeParams $ingestOwn

        $invokeDict = New-ForwardedParamDictionary -TargetCommand 'Invoke-Plan' `
            -ExcludeParams ($ingestOwn + @('Items', 'Plan'))

        foreach ($key in $invokeDict.Keys)
        {
            if (-not $dict.ContainsKey($key)) { $dict[$key] = $invokeDict[$key] }
        }

        return $dict
    }

    process
    {
        $skipped = [List[PSCustomObject]]::new()
        $warnings = [List[string]]::new()
        $errors = [List[string]]::new()

        # Normalize graph input
        $graphData = $FilteredFsGraph
        if ($FilteredFsGraph -is [PSCustomObject] -and
            $null -ne $FilteredFsGraph.PSObject.Properties['Graph'])
        {
            $graphData = $FilteredFsGraph.Graph
            if ($FilteredFsGraph.Skipped)
            {
                foreach ($s in $FilteredFsGraph.Skipped) { $skipped.Add($s) }
            }
        }
        $graphNodes = if ($graphData -is [System.Collections.IDictionary])
        { $graphData.Values } else { $graphData }

        # Collect eligible file descriptors
        $eligible = [List[object]]::new()
        foreach ($node in $graphNodes)
        {
            if (-not $node.Files) { continue }
            foreach ($f in $node.Files) { $eligible.Add($f) }
        }

        if ($eligible.Count -eq 0)
        {
            return [PSCustomObject]@{
                Results  = @()
                Skipped  = $skipped.ToArray()
                Errors   = $errors.ToArray()
                Warnings = $warnings.ToArray()
                Streams  = @()
                Budget   = $null
                Timing   = $null
            }
        }

        # Partition forwarded parameters
        $compileParams = @((Get-Command 'Compile-Plan').Parameters.Keys)
        $dispatchOnly = @((Get-Command 'Invoke-Plan').Parameters.Keys |
                Where-Object { $_ -notin $compileParams })

        $compileSplat = Split-ForwardedParams -BoundParameters $PSBoundParameters `
            -OwnParams (@('FilteredFsGraph') + $dispatchOnly)

        $dispatchSplat = Split-ForwardedParams -BoundParameters $PSBoundParameters `
            -OwnParams (@('FilteredFsGraph') + $compileParams)

        # Stage 1: Compile colonel plan
        $compiled = Compile-Plan @compileSplat

        foreach ($w in $compiled.Warnings) { $warnings.Add($w) }

        if ($compiled.Errors.Count -gt 0)
        {
            foreach ($e in $compiled.Errors) { $errors.Add($e) }
            return [PSCustomObject]@{
                Results  = @()
                Skipped  = $skipped.ToArray()
                Errors   = $errors.ToArray()
                Warnings = $warnings.ToArray()
                Streams  = @()
                Budget   = $null
                Timing   = $null
            }
        }

        # Stage 2: Dispatch via colonel
        $dispatchSplat['Items'] = $eligible.ToArray()
        $dispatchSplat['Plan'] = $compiled.Plan

        $result = Invoke-Plan @dispatchSplat

        foreach ($w in $result.Warnings) { $warnings.Add($w) }
        foreach ($e in $result.Errors) { $errors.Add($e) }

        return [PSCustomObject]@{
            Results  = $result.Results
            Skipped  = $skipped.ToArray()
            Errors   = $errors.ToArray()
            Warnings = $warnings.ToArray()
            Streams  = @($result.Streams)
            Budget   = $result.Budget
            Timing   = $result.Timing
        }
    }
}
#endregion

Export-ModuleMember -Function 'Invoke-Ingest'

    foreach ($step in $Steps)
    {
        if ([string]::IsNullOrWhiteSpace($step.Key))
        {
            $errors.Add('A step has an empty or missing Key.')
            continue
        }
        [void]$referencedKeys.Add($step.Key)
    }

    foreach ($key in $referencedKeys)
    {
        if (-not $Manifest.ContainsKey($key))
        {
            $errors.Add("Step references processor key '$key' which is absent from the manifest.")
        }
    }

    if ($errors.Count -gt 0)
    {
        return [pscustomobject]@{ Plan = $null; Errors = $errors.ToArray(); Warnings = $warnings.ToArray() }
    }

    # Parallel bootstrap read + validate
    $entriesToRead = @($Manifest.GetEnumerator() | Where-Object { $referencedKeys.Contains($_.Key) })

    $batches = [System.Collections.Generic.List[object[]]]::new()
    for ($i = 0; $i -lt $entriesToRead.Count; $i += $InitThreads)
    {
        $end = [Math]::Min($i + $InitThreads, $entriesToRead.Count) - 1
        $batches.Add($entriesToRead[$i..$end])
    }

    $loadedBodies = [System.Collections.Generic.List[hashtable]]::new($entriesToRead.Count)
    $readScript = $script:ReadProcessorScript

    foreach ($batch in $batches)
    {
        $wave = [System.Collections.Generic.List[hashtable]]::new($batch.Count)
        foreach ($entry in $batch)
        {
            $ps = [PowerShell]::Create()
            $null = $ps.AddScript($readScript).AddArgument([string]$entry.Key).AddArgument([string]$entry.Value)
            $async = $ps.BeginInvoke()
            $wave.Add(@{ PS = $ps; Async = $async })
        }
        foreach ($reader in $wave)
        {
            try
            {
                $res = $reader.PS.EndInvoke($reader.Async)
                if ($res -and $res.Count -gt 0) { $loadedBodies.Add($res[0]) }
            }
            catch { $warnings.Add("Bootstrap reader threw: $($_.Exception.Message)") }
            finally { $reader.PS.Dispose() }
        }
    }

    # Validate loaded bodies
    $validBodies = @{}
    foreach ($loaded in $loadedBodies)
    {
        if ($loaded.Error) { $errors.Add("Processor '$($loaded.Key)': $($loaded.Error)"); continue }
        $validBodies[$loaded.Key] = $loaded
    }

    if ($errors.Count -gt 0)
    {
        return [pscustomobject]@{ Plan = $null; Errors = $errors.ToArray(); Warnings = $warnings.ToArray() }
    }

    # Serial ISS construction
    $iss = Build-Iss -Preset $IssPreset -Modules $IssModules

        $procPath = [string]$Manifest[$key]
        $procDir = if ($procPath) { [System.IO.Path]::GetDirectoryName($procPath) } else { '' }
        $cfgPath = if ($procDir) { Join-Path $procDir "configs\$key.json" } else { '' }

        $effectiveConfig = @{}
        if ($cfgPath -and [System.IO.File]::Exists($cfgPath))
        {
            try
            {
                $json = [System.IO.File]::ReadAllText($cfgPath)
                $fileDefaults = $json | ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable
                if ($null -ne $fileDefaults)
                {
                    foreach ($k in $fileDefaults.Keys) { $effectiveConfig[$k] = $fileDefaults[$k] }
                }
            }
            catch
            {
                $warnings.Add("Compile-Plan: failed to parse config file '$cfgPath': $($_.Exception.Message)")
            }
        }

        if ($null -ne $step.Config)
        {
            if ($step.Config -is [System.Collections.IDictionary])
            {
                foreach ($k in $step.Config.Keys) { $effectiveConfig[$k] = $step.Config[$k] }
            }
            elseif ($step.Config -is [System.Management.Automation.PSCustomObject])
            {
                foreach ($p in $step.Config.PSObject.Properties) { $effectiveConfig[$p.Name] = $p.Value }
            }
        }

        [pscustomobject]@{
            Key    = $key
            Fn     = [string]$validBodies[$key].Fn
            Config = $effectiveConfig
        }
    }

    return [pscustomobject]@{
        Plan     = [pscustomobject]@{
            Steps         = @($boundSteps)
            Iss           = $iss
            ProcessorKeys = @($referencedKeys)
        }
        Errors   = @()
        Warnings = $warnings.ToArray()
    }

Agreed — and the rename is the right call; `IncludeProcessors` says set-not-sequence in the name. Let me read the remaining seams so the map names real lines.
Everything's read. Here's the map.

## Shape of the change

Three decisions drive it, all vetoable:

**A new module holds the pure resolvers.** `rs.core.sequencing.psm1` — load/validate, enable-closure, class detection, variant resolution. Pure functions over hashtables, so the richest test surface in the whole job needs no runspaces, no filesystem, no dispatch.

**`Compile-Plan` orchestrates; `-Steps` survives as the verbatim path.** It gains a sequencing parameter set and keeps the existing one. Either way the Plan carries `Variants` + `Routing` — a verbatim compile is just a family of one named `default` with an empty routing map. `Invoke-Plan` therefore never learns modes exist, and [colonel-validation.tests.ps1](utils/reposnapshot/tests/colonel-validation.tests.ps1) keeps passing untouched because it drives `-Steps`.

**Ingest computes the corpus extension set.** [rs.core.ingest.psm1:81](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.ingest.psm1:81) is where `$eligible` fills; the distinct `Extension` set falls out there and rides into `$compileSplat` at [:102](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.ingest.psm1:102). Everything else in that module is forwarding and stays as-is.

## Phase 1 — sequencing module

New `rs.core.sequencing.psm1`, nothing wired:

| Function | Contract |
|---|---|
| `Import-Sequencing` | path → normalized `@{Processors; Routing}`, ints cast, extensions normalized to leading-dot lowercase, all validations hard-error |
| `Resolve-EnabledSet` | `IncludeProcessors` ∪ `Default:true`, then `Requires` closure → set |
| `Resolve-Classes` | enabled tokens + corpus extensions → class list (`default` included only if some extension is unclaimed) |
| `Resolve-Variants` | enabled set + classes + routing → `@{class → ordered @{Key;File}}`, sorted by `(Group, Rank)`, tokens spliced when unresolved |

The validations to enforce — the conventions we settled, plus what the recipe implies:

1. JSON parses; `Group`/`Rank` parse as ints.
2. Every non-token key resolves to `processors\<key>.ps1`; every token key (`$`-prefixed) has a `Routing` entry, and vice versa.
3. Every route's `processor` file exists on disk.
4. **Rank-0 exclusivity** — a group holding a Rank-0 member holds only that member; otherwise ranks within a group are distinct.
5. **`Requires` points backward** — for each edge X→Y, Y's `(Group, Rank)` sorts before X's. Closure and ordering are separate mechanisms here, so a forward edge would enable Y and then run it too late, silently.
6. **Extension → language is globally unique across tokens.** This is the one place the recipe is single-token-shaped: `Resolve-Steps` keys on a single `$class` string, which stays well-defined only if `.ps1` never means `powershell` under one token and something else under another. Enforcing uniqueness globally keeps a second token additive rather than a rework.

New `tests/sequencing.tests.ps1` covering each validation negatively, plus: closure pulls `file-read` in; array position in `IncludeProcessors` is ignored; `.cs` in the corpus produces a `csharp` variant; permuting `Processors` keys changes no resolved chain while editing `Group`/`Rank` does.

Battery stays green — nothing consumes this yet.

## Phase 2 — Compile-Plan emits the family

[rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1):

- **:139** — `-Steps` joins a parameter set; add `-SequencingPath`, `-IncludeProcessors`, `-Extensions`.
- **:164-181** — `$referencedKeys` becomes the union across variants. The HashSet already does this correctly once it iterates every variant's steps.
- **:269-311** — the bind loop is per-step and variant-agnostic; it runs once per variant. Config deferral needs no change at all: `configs\$key.json` picks up `rs.ps.strip.json` because the step's `Key` is the resolved basename.
- **:316-321** — Plan gains `Variants` and `Routing`, and **keeps `Steps`** (the `default` variant) this phase so `Invoke-Plan` is untouched and the battery stays green.

## Phase 3 — Invoke-Plan per-item dispatch

- **:437-449** — third parallel array beside `$sliceItems`/`$sliceIdxs`, filled at slice time with `$Plan.Routing[$Items[$i].Extension]`, defaulting to `default`. Because extensions were normalized at load to the crawler's leading-dot form ([rs.core.crawler.psm1:132](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.crawler.psm1:132)), this is a direct dictionary hit with no per-item string work.
- **:531-533** — `$planStepsForWorker` becomes a keyed family, same minimal-shape projection, marshalled once and shared by reference.
- **:547** — one more `AddArgument` for the slice's variant keys.
- **:508-529** — worker prebuilds `variantKey → plan` once, then `$plans[$MyPlanKeys[$i]]` per item. The `Invoke-ChainExecutor` call is byte-identical.
- Drop `Plan.Steps` now that nothing reads it.

Test gate: mixed-corpus index stability, and each item's `Processing` trail proving it ran its assigned chain.

## Phase 4 — user CLI

[rs.core.user.ps1](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1):

- **:101** — `-Processors` → `-IncludeProcessors`; add `-RunVerbatim`.
- **:175** — config key follows the rename. This is the line that currently makes your `IncludeProcessors` inert.
- **:219-225** — manifest build unchanged (`deferred/` is already outside the non-recursive glob).
- **:226-245** — the whole steps block goes: the hard-prepended `file-read`, the explicit branch, the default branch. `Default: true` in sequencing replaces the prepend; the default branch's `rs-whitespace` + conditional `rs-content_meta` is now expressed by `IncludeProcessors`.
- **:126** — `ConvertTo-ProcessorStep` stays, reachable only under `-RunVerbatim`.
- **:248-259** — cautions split: silent under sequencing (compiler guarantees), retained under `-RunVerbatim`.
- **:261** — the `Invoke-Ingest` call swaps `-Steps` for the sequencing params.
- [user-config.json](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/user-config.json)'s `$comment` updates: bare keys defer to `configs/<key>.json`, `$strip` defers to its resolved processor's config.

## Phase 5 — echo, and docs

Per-variant ConfigEcho keeping the three stories distinct. And the doc reconciliation: I'd `git mv` [docs/spine-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/spine-and-routing.md) to `docs/sequencing-and-routing.md` and rewrite it to the Group/Rank model — the two-layer contract, dispatch, echo, and most invariants survive verbatim; `Spine`/`Languages`/registry-filename sections get replaced. Your `issues/` doc stays the algorithmic recipe; the `docs/` one stays the durable doctrine, indexed from [docs/README.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/README.md).

# no we don't need a new RS.core file. this either lives in the colonel since that's colonels job, or in rs.core.user

Colonel — it's plan compilation, which is Compile-Plan's job by definition, and invariant 1 says the compiler alone reads the sequencing file. `rs.core.user` is resolution precedence and config lifecycle; the steps-building block there is precisely what Phase 4 deletes.

And there's already a precedent for the shape: colonel exports `Resolve-WorkerBudget` and `Build-Iss` as granular pure functions beside the two big entry points ([colonel.v2:671-676](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:671)). The resolvers join that export list rather than inventing a module:

- `Import-Sequencing`, `Resolve-EnabledSet`, `Resolve-Classes`, `Resolve-Variants` — added to [rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1) in their own region above `Compile-Plan`, exported, pure over hashtables except `Import-Sequencing`'s single read.
- File I/O in colonel is already established — Compile-Plan reads processor scripts, `configs/*.json`, and chain-executor.
- Tests become `tests/colonel-sequencing.tests.ps1`, matching the existing `colonel-validation` / `colonel-dispatch` naming, and added to the run-all roster.

One knock-on: `-SequencingPath` becomes an explicit parameter on Compile-Plan, following how `-ChainExecutorPath` and `-SharedHelperPath` are passed today from [rs.core.user.ps1:261-263](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:261), rather than colonel deriving it. (It *could* derive it from a manifest value the way the config path is derived at [:271](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:271), but explicit matches the surrounding convention and keeps colonel free of layout assumptions.)

Phases 2–5 are unchanged; Phase 1 just becomes "add the four resolvers plus tests to colonel," still behavior-neutral since nothing calls them until Phase 2.

#region Resolve-WorkerBudget
function Resolve-WorkerBudget
{
    <#
    .SYNOPSIS
        Determines thread count and allocation policy for a batch.
    #>
    param(
        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [int]           $ItemCount,
        [nullable[int]]                        $MaxWorkers = $null,
        [int]                                  $ReservedCores = 2,
        [int]                                  $MinItemsPerWorker = 4
    )

    $warnings = [System.Collections.Generic.List[string]]::new()
    $logical = [Math]::Max(1, [Environment]::ProcessorCount)

    if ($null -ne $MaxWorkers)
    {
        $policy = 'Explicit'
        if ($MaxWorkers -gt $logical)
        {
            $warnings.Add("MaxWorkers ($MaxWorkers) exceeds logical core count ($logical); clamping.")
            $MaxWorkers = $logical
        }
        $ceiling = [Math]::Max(1, $MaxWorkers)
    }
    else
    {
        $policy = 'Auto'
        $reserved = [Math]::Min($ReservedCores, $logical - 1)
        $ceiling = [Math]::Max(1, $logical - $reserved)
    }

    $graded = if ($MinItemsPerWorker -gt 0 -and $ItemCount -gt 0)
    {
        [Math]::Max(1, [int][Math]::Ceiling($ItemCount / $MinItemsPerWorker))
    }
    else { $ceiling }

    $threads = [Math]::Min($ceiling, $graded)
    $threads = [Math]::Max(1, [Math]::Min($threads, [Math]::Max(1, $ItemCount)))

    return [pscustomobject]@{
        Threads  = $threads
        Policy   = $policy
        Warnings = $warnings.ToArray()
        Inputs   = [pscustomobject]@{
            ItemCount         = $ItemCount
            MaxWorkers        = $MaxWorkers
            ReservedCores     = $ReservedCores
            MinItemsPerWorker = $MinItemsPerWorker
            LogicalCores      = $logical
        }
    }
}
#endregion

    #>

$script:Passed = 0
$script:Failed = 0

function Enter-Section ([string]$Name)
{
    Write-Host "`n── $Name" -ForegroundColor Cyan
}

function Assert-True ([bool]$Condition, [string]$Label, [string]$Detail = '')
{
    if ($Condition)
    {
        $script:Passed++
        Write-Host "    PASS  $Label" -ForegroundColor Green
    }
    else
    {
        $script:Failed++
        $msg = "    FAIL  $Label"
        if ($Detail) { $msg += "  ($Detail)" }
        Write-Host $msg -ForegroundColor Red
    }
}

{

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Fixture processor scripts
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
$fixtureRoot = Join-Path ([IO.Path]::GetTempPath()) "rs-colonel-val-$([guid]::NewGuid().ToString('N').Substring(0,8))"
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $fixtureRoot | Out-Null

Set-Content -Path (Join-Path $fixtureRoot 'wrapper.ps1') -Value @'
=== tail ===
    Assert-True ($run.Results[0].Doubled -eq 42) 'helper executed: 21 → 42' "got $($run.Results[0].Doubled)"
    Assert-True ($run.Results[1].Doubled -eq 10) 'helper executed: 5 → 10'
    Assert-True ($run.Results[0].Value -eq 21) 'input properties preserved through copy'
}
catch
{
    # A terminating error inside the try block — a StrictMode property access, a
    # parameter-binding failure — would otherwise abort the suite SILENTLY:
    # finally runs, execution resumes after the block, and the summary prints a
    # PASSING count while the remaining asserts never ran. That mode is invisible
    # from outside (tests/run-all.ps1 cannot detect it — the counts are
    # self-consistent), so it has to be caught HERE.
    Assert-True $false "SUITE ABORTED: $($_.Exception.Message)" $_.ScriptStackTrace
}
finally
{
    Remove-Item -LiteralPath $fixtureRoot -Recurse -Force -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
}

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
Write-Host "`n═══ colonel-validation: $script:Passed passed, $script:Failed failed ═══" -ForegroundColor $(if ($script:Failed -eq 0) { 'Green' } else { 'Red' })
if ($script:Failed -gt 0) { exit 1 }

Starting Phase 1. Adding the resolver region to colonel:
Now the test suite:
Heredoc truncated mid-file. Let me check the module-import convention and write it with the file tool instead.
Five failures, three of them my test assertions indexing a single-element result that PowerShell unwrapped to a scalar, and two a real module bug: `$x = if (...) { @() }` yields `$null`, because an if-statement emitting an empty array emits nothing. Fixing both.
Now adding it to the roster and running the full battery:
Phase 1 landed (`0998534`). The four resolvers are in colonel beside `Resolve-WorkerBudget`, exported, with nothing calling them yet — runtime behavior is unchanged and the battery is green at **24 suites / 1460 asserts**, the new suite contributing 43.

**What the suite pins.** Section 2 is the interesting one — every declared convention as a negative test, each expected to be terminating: non-integer `Group`, unknown fixed key, token without a `Routing` table, `Routing` for an undeclared token, a route naming an absent processor, a route with no extensions, an extension claimed by two classes, Rank-0 sitting beside other members, duplicate ranks in a group, a `Requires` edge pointing forward, and `Requires` naming an unknown entry. One of those is your own bug from two turns ago: **"route filename that disagrees with disk rejected"** covers `rs.cs.strip.cs`, which passes a basename check but fails against the file the manifest actually holds.

Sections 4–6 pin the behaviors we argued through: an all-PowerShell corpus compiles *no* default variant, a disabled token claims nothing so everything falls to default, the default chain is exactly one step shorter than powershell's with no tombstones, and permuting `Processors` keys changes no compiled chain while swapping `Rank` values does.

**Two bugs surfaced by running it.** A real one in the module: `$exts = if (...) { @(...) } else { @() }` lands as `$null`, because an if-branch yielding an empty array emits nothing to the pipeline. It bit `extensions` and would have bitten an empty `Requires` array; both are now direct assignments with a comment saying why. And three of my own assertions indexed `[0]` into results PowerShell had unwrapped to scalars — `$bare[0]` was returning `'f'`, not `'file-read'`.

**One deletion to flag:** I removed `processors.registry.json`, since you described `sequencing.json` as its rename and the old file was left orphaned. Say the word if you wanted it kept.

Also untracked and left alone: `tests/languages/` appeared in the working tree — yours, I assume.

Phase 2 next: `Compile-Plan` gains `-SequenceManifest`, unions `$referencedKeys` across variants, binds each variant through the existing loop, and returns `Variants` + `Routing` while still emitting `Steps` so `Invoke-Plan` stays untouched and the battery stays green.

Good — and `tests/languages/` is exactly the fixture Phase 4's e2e gate wants, since routing correctness is only observable across a heterogeneous corpus. Let me see what's there, then align the keyspace.
Seven languages, two with strippers — that's 2 routed classes plus a default covering js/nu/py/rs/ts, which is exactly the shape Phase 4 needs. Aligning the route keyspace to stubs:
Committed (`52260f8`). Routes now name keys (`"processor": "rs.ps.strip"`), matched by plain membership against the manifest — which is itself derived from the on-disk stubs, so as you said, a correctly named processor can't miss. The filename-versus-disk reconciliation is gone entirely; there's no second spelling to keep in agreement, and `Routing`, `Processors` keys, `configs/<key>.json`, and `IncludeProcessors` are now one keyspace.

The typo class it was catching is still caught, just by a simpler rule — three tests cover it: a value carrying an extension (`rs.cs.strip.ps1`), a mistyped one (`rs.cs.strip.cs`), and an absent one (`rs.no.such`) all fail identically as "not a processor key," with the known keys listed. Suite is 44 asserts, still green.

**One doc drift to note:** your [sequencing-routing.md](issues/reposnapshot/design/sequencing-routing.md) still shows `$file = $seq.Routing[$k][$class].processor   # rs.ps.strip.ps1` and derives the key via `GetFileNameWithoutExtension`. Both collapse to a direct `.Key` read now. I left your doc alone — say the word if you want it updated.

**On the fixture:** seven languages with specimens, two routed. That gives Phase 4's e2e exactly the shape that makes routing observable — a powershell variant, a csharp variant, and a default covering js/nu/py/rs/ts, with per-file `Processing` trails proving each got its own chain rather than a shared one. Worth noting `bash/` is empty, and `typescript/safe-shell.d.ts` will land in the default class as `.d.ts` normalizes to `.ts`... actually it normalizes to the extension `.ts`, so it routes wherever `.ts` does — nowhere today, so default either way.

Phase 2 next: `Compile-Plan` gains `-SequenceManifest`, unions `$referencedKeys` across variants, binds each through the existing loop, and returns `Variants` + `Routing` while still emitting `Steps` so `Invoke-Plan` stays untouched.

# in order to slice on a property with a hyphen you have to single quote it e.g. Processors.'file-read' i believe

**[result: toolu_01R6GYGJZS...]**
```
--- exit: clean if empty above ---
```

Right — `$obj.file-read` parses `-read` as a token, so it needs `$obj.'file-read'` (or `."file-read"`, or `$obj.$name`). Let me confirm nothing in what I wrote relies on bare dot access to a hyphenated name.
Clean — every hyphenated key in both files goes through bracket indexing (`$procs[$key]`, `$shipped.Processors['rs-indent']`), never bare dot access. Two things make that hold rather than being luck:

`ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable` is load-bearing here. Without it the manifest would come back as `PSCustomObject`, and then every processor key — `rs-indent`, `rs-content_meta`, `file-read` — becomes a property name needing `.'rs-indent'` at every access site, plus `.psobject.Properties` gymnastics to enumerate. As hashtables, keys are just strings and bracket indexing is uniform. Worth keeping in mind as the reason, not just the habit.

Where it *will* bite is later phases. Any structure keyed by processor key that gets projected to `PSCustomObject` inherits the problem — the Phase 5 echo is the obvious candidate if it tabulates per-step data as an object rather than a list of records. The dodge is to make such structures arrays of `@{Key=…; …}` records rather than objects keyed by name, which also keys by processor key rather than position, satisfying invariant 4 at the same time.

Also worth noting for interactive poking at the file: `$seq.Processors.'$strip'` needs single quotes for a second reason — `"$strip"` in double quotes interpolates to empty, which is invariant 7's hazard showing up at the console rather than in source.

    $rawProcs = $raw['Processors']
    $rawRouting = if ($raw.Contains('Routing') -and $null -ne $raw['Routing']) { $raw['Routing'] } else { @{} }

    # Normalize entries; Group and Rank are ordinals, so they must parse as integers.
    $procs = @{}
    foreach ($key in $rawProcs.Keys)
    {
        $e = $rawProcs[$key]
        if ($e -isnot [System.Collections.IDictionary])
        {
            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: entry '$key' is not an object."
        }

        $group = 0
        $rank = 0
        $gTxt = if ($e.Contains('Group')) { [string]$e['Group'] } else { '' }
        $rTxt = if ($e.Contains('Rank')) { [string]$e['Rank'] } else { '' }
        if (-not [int]::TryParse($gTxt, [ref]$group))
        {
            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: '$key' has a missing or non-integer Group ('$gTxt')."
        }
        if (-not [int]::TryParse($rTxt, [ref]$rank))
        {
            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: '$key' has a missing or non-integer Rank ('$rTxt')."
        }

        # Direct assignment, not an if-expression: a branch yielding an empty array
        # emits nothing, and the variable would land as $null instead of @().
        $requires = @()
        if ($e.Contains('Requires') -and $null -ne $e['Requires']) { $requires = @([string[]]$e['Requires']) }

        $procs[$key] = @{
            Key      = $key
            Group    = $group
            Rank     = $rank
            Default  = if ($e.Contains('Default')) { [bool]$e['Default'] } else { $false }
            Requires = $requires
            IsToken  = $key.StartsWith('$')
        }
    }

    # A fixed key names its own processor file; a token names none and routes instead.
    foreach ($key in $procs.Keys)
    {
        if ($procs[$key].IsToken)
        {
            if (-not $rawRouting.Contains($key))
            {
                throw "Import-SequenceManifest: token '$key' has no Routing table."
            }
        }
        elseif (-not $Manifest.ContainsKey($key))
        {
            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: '$key' has no processors\$key.ps1 (known: $($Manifest.Keys -join ', '))."
        }
    }
    foreach ($token in $rawRouting.Keys)
    {
        if (-not $procs.ContainsKey($token))
        {
            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: Routing declares '$token', which has no Processors entry."
        }
        if (-not $procs[$token].IsToken)

            throw "Import-SequenceManifest: Routing declares '$token', which is not a token."
        }
    }

    # Routes bind to real processor files; extensions normalize to the crawler's
    # leading-dot form and must name at most one class across the whole manifest.

        $langs = @{}

        {
            $route = $rawRouting[$token][$lang]
            if ($route -isnot [System.Collections.IDictionary] -or
                -not $route.Contains('processor') -or
                [string]::IsNullOrWhiteSpace([string]$route['processor']))
            {
                throw "Import-SequenceManifest: route $token/$lang names no processor."
            }

            # A route names a processor key, never a filename: the manifest derives
            # its keys from the same on-disk stubs, so a correctly named processor
            # cannot miss, and there is no second spelling to keep in agreement.
            $rKey = [string]$route['processor']
            if (-not $Manifest.ContainsKey($rKey))
            {
                throw "Import-SequenceManifest: route $token/$lang names '$rKey', which is not a processor key (known: $($Manifest.Keys -join ', '))."
            }

Substantial reshape. Let me read the new state before touching anything.
Digested. The sigil is gone — routed-ness is now structural (`Routing` array vs `File`), which dissolves the `"$strip"` interpolation hazard entirely, and there are no language names at all, so a file class is now purely its resolution tuple. Let me enumerate what the renames leave pointing at old paths.
Let me re-read my region exactly before reshaping it.
Now reshaping the loader for the new structure — routed-ness by `Routing` vs `File`, and variant identity as the resolution tuple since there are no language names to key on.
Now rewriting the test suite for the new shape:
48/48 green against the new shape. Now the battery, to enumerate what the renames left dangling:
Loader is green; the battery shows the rename fallout. Let me see what's staged versus in-flight before committing only my files.
The renames are already on main; only four files are in flight. Committing the reshape together, since colonel now depends on the new sequencer format:
Loader reshaped and committed (`ada361e`) — **48 asserts, green on first run** against the new format.

**What the new shape bought.** Routed-ness is structural (`Routing` array vs `File`), so the `$` sigil is gone and invariant 7's interpolation hazard with it. And since the sequencer names no languages, a file class *is* its resolution tuple — `Resolve-Classes` became `Resolve-Routing`, which maps each corpus extension to a variant key built from the routes claiming it. Tuple-collapse now falls out for free rather than needing a dedup pass: `.ps1`/`.psm1`/`.psd1` share one compiled chain because they resolve identically, and `.md`/`.py` collapse onto one `default`. Occupancy is decided on the route records exactly as you specified; the flattened union only ever answers "claimed at all," which is just `$parts.Count -eq 0`.

Extension uniqueness moved from global to **per-slot** — with no language names, two different slots claiming `.ps1` is legitimate and expected, so only two routes *within* a slot collide.

## Alignment list

The battery is red — **13 suites, 114 failures**, all downstream of the file renames, none from the sequencer work:

**Runtime, in `rs.core.user.ps1`:** the manifest exclusion list at [:223](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:223) still names `chain-executor.ps1`/`bag-helpers.ps1`, so both now get treated as processors; `-ChainExecutorPath`/`-SharedHelperPath` at [:262](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:262) point at files that no longer exist; the default-chain branch and the three cautions still use `rs-whitespace`/`rs-content_meta` keys.

**Config deferral:** `configs/rs-indent.json` and `configs/rs-whitespace.json` are the last two hyphenated files. `Compile-Plan` derives `configs\<stub>.json`, so they need to become `rs.indent.json`/`rs.whitespace.json` — the eleven pre-staged stripper configs already match.

**Processor self-names:** [rs.indent.ps1:16](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/rs.indent.ps1:16) and [rs.whitespace.ps1:15](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/rs.whitespace.ps1:15) call `Resolve-ProcessorConfig -ProcessorName 'rs-indent'` / `'rs-whitespace'`, which is a second config lookup path needing the same stub.

**Tests:** processor suites still reference old paths and keys — `rs-content_meta.tests.ps1` hand-builds a manifest with `file-read`/`rs-content_meta` plus the old executor/helper paths — and the suite files themselves are still hyphenated, as is the run-all roster.

**Stale but not runtime:** `contracts/rs-content_meta.contract.json`, `contracts/ingest.contract.json`, and the `.LINK docs/<old>.md` headers in every renamed processor.

**One decision inside that sweep, not a rename.** The `Processing` trail emits `Processor = 'rs-indent'` — that's payload a reading agent consumes, so it's worth deciding rather than sed-ing: the file stub (`rs.indent`, traceable to what ran) or the semantic slot (`Indentation`, stable across a processor swap). A processor can't know its own slot, so slot-naming would have to be attached by the executor from the step's `Slot` field, which the variants already carry.

Want me to run that sweep? It's mechanical apart from that one call.

    # *.contract.json, excluding rs-*.contract.json — contracts/ also holds
    # payload declarations (container.spec.jsonc, excluded by extension) and
    # processor producer-contracts (rs.content_meta.contract.json and any
    # future rs-* sibling), which declare { processor, in, out } rather than
    # { stage, in, out } and must not be parsed as a stage contract.
    $files = @(Get-ChildItem -LiteralPath $schemaDir -Filter '*.contract.json' -Exclude 'rs-*.contract.json' | Sort-Object Name)
    Assert-True ($files.Count -ge 1) "contract files found under contracts/" "got $($files.Count)"

    $contracts = @{}
    foreach ($f in $files)
    {
        $c = Get-Content -LiteralPath $f.FullName -Raw | ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable
        $expected = $f.Name -replace '\.contract\.json$', ''
        Assert-True ($c.stage -eq $expected) "$($f.Name): stage = '$expected'" "got '$($c.stage)'"
        Assert-True ($c.ContainsKey('in') -and $c.ContainsKey('out')) "$($f.Name): has in and out"
        $contracts[$c.stage] = $c
    }

**[result: toolu_017So9pNhR...]**
```
    $file = $split[0]
    $segs = @(($split[1].TrimStart('/')) -split '/' | Where-Object { $_ -ne '' })

    $target = Join-Path $SpecDir $file
    if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $target))
    {
        $target = Join-Path (Split-Path -Parent $SpecDir) $file
        if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $target)) { throw "rs.core.container: '$Ref' at $Where names a contract that does not exist ($file)." }
    }
    if (-not $script:ContractCache.ContainsKey($target))
    {
        $script:ContractCache[$target] = Get-Content -LiteralPath $target -Raw | ConvertFrom-Json -AsHashtable
    }
    $node = $script:ContractCache[$target]
    foreach ($s in $segs)
    {
        $node = Get-Prop $node $s
        if ($null -eq $node) { throw "rs.core.container: '$Ref' at $Where does not resolve — $file has no /$($segs -join '/')." }
    }

    switch -Wildcard ($file)
    {
        'assemble.contract.json'  { return 'entry.' + (@($segs[3..($segs.Count - 1)]) -join '.') }
        'shards.contract.json'    { return 'plan.'  + $segs[-1] }
        'container.contract.json' { return 'codec.' + $segs[-1] }
        'rs-*.contract.json'      { return 'entry.' + (@($segs[1..($segs.Count - 1)]) -join '.') }
        default { throw "rs.core.container: '$Ref' at $Where points at $file, which has no accessor derivation." }
    }
}
#endregion

~35 files. Renames first (not source edits), then content one file at a time.
Now content, starting with the runtime-critical file:
Now the processors themselves — self-names and doc links:
Now the processor test suites:
Now the main test suites:
Runtime and tests swept. Let me verify before moving to docs:
6 failures left, but note the assert counts dropped in some suites (contracts 135→14, container 81→8) — those are aborting early. Let me see the actual errors:
Two misses: `.jsonc` wasn't in my grep's include list, and the contract file's own content still self-names. Let me look:
Second glob-pattern miss — wildcards don't show up in literal greps. Fixing, then sweeping for the pattern class itself:
All remaining `rs-` hits are temp-dir prefixes, a deprecated bench docstring, and `rs-nonexistent` (a deliberately invalid name in a negative test). One stale comment line left:
Green — **24 suites, 1465 asserts, 0 failures**, with assert counts back at pre-rename levels (contracts 135, container 81, selfie 25, user 26), so the suites are running to completion rather than aborting early. Committing the code sweep, then docs:
Docs sweep. Let me check AGENTS.md first — it has a deliberate historical path reference that must stay:
Sweep complete, in two commits — code (`37a6ed9`) and docs (`2c9b254`). **Battery green: 24 suites, 1465 asserts, 0 failures.**

**Renames** (via `git mv`, so history follows): the two hyphenated configs → `rs.indent.json`/`rs.whitespace.json`, six per-processor docs, four test suites (including `rs-numerics` → `rs.numerics`, which you hadn't flagged but was the same convention), and the content_meta contract.

**Two misses worth calling out, both invisible to a literal grep** — they were wildcard patterns, not names:

- `contracts.tests.ps1` excluded processor contracts with `-Exclude 'rs-*.contract.json'`. After the rename that glob stopped matching, so the processor contract got parsed as a stage contract and the suite aborted.
- `rs.core.container.psm1:190` derives `$ref` accessors through `switch -Wildcard` with a `'rs-*.contract.json'` arm, so every `content_meta` sub-field ref failed to resolve.

Both showed up as *early suite aborts* rather than assert failures — contracts dropped 135→13, container 81→7 — which is the signature the run-all catch exists to expose. Worth noting because the same trap will recur: a rename sweep should grep for the pattern class (`rs-*`, `rs-?`) alongside the literal names.

**Deliberately left alone:** the `git log 58a1a26 -- .../rs-psstrip.ps1` example in AGENTS.md, which points at a historical path on purpose; `rs-nonexistent` in a negative test; and `rs-*` temp-directory prefixes, which name nothing.

**One item still open:** [docs/spine-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/spine-and-routing.md) is untouched and now doubly superseded — it describes a `Spine` array, `Languages`, `$`-sigil tokens, and `processors.registry.json`, none of which exist. I didn't de-stale it because polishing names in a doc that should be retired is wasted work. Your call: archive it (with `docs/README.md` losing the entry), or rewrite it as the durable architecture doctrine for the slot/Group/Rank model with your `issues/` doc staying the algorithmic recipe.

# indiscriminate grepping and getting hits in a git log for something like this where one is trying to repair current code is exactly the kind of self-inflicted context thrashing that I hate about grep in general and model's reflexive use of it for everything. there must be a smarter way to have approached the problem you were faced when you used it. you don't need a git log to find occurences of strings in current source code. grep searches should be predicated and thoughtful, done iteratively over meaningful subsets of the search space, such as documentation vs source code vs git logs. but i digress. the grep blindspot exposes a stupid wildcard pattern, how do we fix that and how do we fix the contracts tests? making updates to fix a something as simple a rename shouldn't be so hard, and that is a smell in its self

Fair on both counts. The better move was to scope by role — source first, fix and verify, then docs as a separate pass — and never let generated `.snapshot/` output or a git-history example into a repair pass. Worth adding: grep couldn't have found those two sites by construction, since the bug *was* that the pattern isn't the name. The battery found them. So for a rename, the verification instrument is the test run, not the search.

On the smell — you're right, and it's one root cause in both places. Let me check whether it's only those two:
That grep was unscoped — my `--` terminated option parsing, so the `--include` filters became file arguments and `.snapshot/` came back in. Redoing it properly:
Exactly two sites, one root cause — and it's the one you're naming: **both infer a file's *kind* from its *name***. The contracts themselves already declare what they are (`stage` vs `processor`), so the filename is redundant information that can silently disagree. Let me see what the test does downstream before rewriting it:
Both fixes are the same change: dispatch on what the contract declares, not what it's called.
Green, and contracts went 135 → **147** asserts — the producer contract is now examined instead of excluded. Let me verify the fix negatively rather than assume it: rename the contract to a name that would *not* have matched the old `rs.*` pattern, and confirm everything still resolves.
Probe confirms it: with the contract named `content_meta.contract.json` — a name the old `rs.*` pattern would never have matched — contracts passes 147, container passes 81 including *"sub-field accessor derived from the processor $ref"*, and selfie's real end-to-end run passes 25. That rename would have thrown `has no accessor derivation` before. Reverting the probe:
Clean revert — only the two fix files remain. Final battery, then commit:
Committed (`d619ecf`). Battery green at **1477**, contracts up 135 → 147.

## The root cause, and why the rename was hard

Both traps were the same mistake: **inferring what a file *is* from what it's *called*.** A contract already declares its own kind — stage contracts name a `stage`, producer contracts name a `processor` — so the filename was redundant information that could silently disagree with the declaration. Once the name is the discriminator, every rename is a semantic change in disguise.

The fix is the same in both places: dispatch on the declaration.

- **`Resolve-RefAccessor`** branches on the loaded contract's `processor`/`stage` rather than matching `$file` against `'rs-*.contract.json'`.
- **`contracts.tests`** partitions the same way instead of excluding producer contracts by glob.

That second one also removes a *test* smell you'd have hit eventually: the old `-Exclude` meant producer contracts were never examined at all, so the suite reported green while silently skipping a file. Now they're parsed and checked, plus a new assert that every contract declares exactly one of `stage`/`processor`, and one that at least one producer was actually examined — so the skip can't come back quietly. Filename-agrees-with-declaration survives as an explicit convention check, which catches a rename that updated only one of the two, but it no longer *decides* anything.

## Verified negatively

I renamed the contract to `content_meta.contract.json` — a name the old `rs.*` pattern could never match — and ran the three suites that consume those refs: contracts 147, container 81 (including *"sub-field accessor derived from the processor $ref"*, the exact path), selfie 25, all green. The old code threw `has no accessor derivation` on precisely that input. Probe reverted; working tree held only the two fix files.

## The general rule this leaves

Identity belongs in content; filenames are addresses, not declarations. A `$ref` still names a file — that's unavoidable, a pointer has to point — but it now fails loudly with *"names a contract that does not exist"* rather than resolving to a wrong accessor shape or a silent skip. That's the distinction worth keeping: **explicit pointers that break loudly are fine; patterns that match names are not.**

On your process point — the lesson I'd take is that grep was the wrong instrument for verifying this rename, since the defect was that the pattern *isn't* the name. Scoped greps are right for the mechanical sweep; the battery is what actually finds the holes, and a scoped search for the *pattern class* (`-Wildcard`, `-Exclude`, `-like` on filenames, source only) is what proves there were exactly two.

**Search the pattern class, not just the names** — `-Wildcard`, `-Exclude`, `-like` on filenames, glob literals. Recorded with the reason it's a genuine blindspot rather than carelessness: the defect is that the pattern isn't the name, so a name search cannot find it by construction.

**The concrete Bash trap** — `grep -rn -- "pattern" --include=*.ps1` silently drops the filter, because `--` ends option parsing and `--include` becomes a file argument. That's the one that made my "scoped" search unscoped mid-conversation, so it's worth the line.

That's the punchline: **`.snapshot/` is already gitignored** ([.gitignore:21](.gitignore:21) `**/.snapshot/**`), and ripgrep honors gitignore by default. So that entire class of noise would never have appeared — it was self-inflicted by shelling out to `grep -r`, which knows nothing about gitignore. And `rg -t ps` covers `*.ps1/*.psm1/*.psd1/*.ps1xml/*.cdxml`, so role-scoping is one flag instead of a hand-rolled `--include` chain. The Grep tool is itself built on ripgrep, so I had this the whole time and reached for Bash `grep` out of exactly the reflex you named.

Updating the memory, since this changes the *how* substantially:
Memory updated. The material change is that ripgrep converts most of that discipline from *something I have to remember* into *tool default*:

- **Gitignore-awareness for free.** `.snapshot/` is gitignored, so rg would have excluded it without my thinking about it. The noise you objected to came entirely from `grep -r` not knowing what a repo is.
- **Role-scoping is one flag.** `rg -t ps` covers the whole PowerShell family; `-t md` for the docs pass. No hand-rolled `--include` chain to get wrong — and no `--` trap to silently drop it.
- **`--no-ignore`** for the rare case where generated output *is* the target, which keeps that an explicit decision rather than an accident.

What ripgrep does **not** fix, and the memory now says so explicitly: the pattern-class blindspot. `switch -Wildcard 'rs-*.contract.json'` is invisible to any search for `rs-content_meta` regardless of engine, because the defect is that the pattern isn't the name. That part still needs the second search (for `-Wildcard`/`-Exclude`/`-like` on filenames) and the battery as the actual verifier.

Worth noting for the ledger: the Grep tool I have available *is* ripgrep, so this capability wasn't missing even before you put `rg` on PATH — I reached for Bash `grep` reflexively instead. Having `rg` on PATH now mainly helps when I genuinely need shell composition (`--type-list`, piping into other commands); for plain searching, the Grep tool is the right call and the memory says to use it first.

# what about pre-tool hook that nudges search in console, beit bash, powershell or nushell

**[tool: Glob]**
```json
{"pattern":"**/.snapshot/**/*_tree.md","path":"D:\\aghado01\\science-facility\\utils\\reposnapshot"}
```

**[result: toolu_01ARYjr3QE...]**
```
utils\reposnapshot\reposnapshot-v3\.snapshot\20260825_001730\reposnapshot-v3_tree.md
utils\reposnapshot\reposnapshot-v3\.snapshot\20260825_001857\reposnapshot-v3_tree.md
```

That's better than my version — it removes the duplicate exclusion list entirely. The hook doesn't need to know *what* to exclude; it just redirects to a tool that already reads your ignore files, so `.gitignore`/`.ignore`/`.rgignore` stay the single source of truth and adding a rule there immediately governs my searches too.

One thing to check before calling it a complete fix — whether Grep is the only leak:
Glob leaks too — same two ignored files. So Grep isn't the only hole, and a Grep-only hook would be a partial fix dressed as a complete one. Hook both:

| Tool call | Console equivalent that honors your ignore files |
|---|---|
| `Grep pattern, path, type/glob` | `rg [-t ps] [-g '*.x'] 'pattern' path` |
| `Glob **/*.ps1` | `rg --files -g '**/*.ps1' path` |

Both routes read `.gitignore`/`.ignore`/`.rgignore` and skip dot-dirs, so your hygiene governs discovery *and* content search.

**Mechanics.** A `PreToolUse` matcher on `Grep|Glob` returns a deny whose reason is the nudge. The valuable part is that the hook can *translate* the call rather than just refusing it — it has the full `tool_input`, so it can hand back the exact command: `output_mode: files_with_matches` → `-l`, `count` → `-c`, `-i/-n/-A/-B/-C` pass straight through, `multiline` → `-U --multiline-dotall`, `type`/`glob` → `-t`/`-g`. I get a runnable line, not a scolding, so there's no flag-mapping for me to get wrong.

**Three decisions that change what I build:**

*Blanket or conditional.* I'd go blanket. A conditional (deny only unscoped calls) sounds friendlier but doesn't restore your control — a *scoped* Grep still ignores your ignore files, so the conditional version leaves the actual complaint unfixed. Blanket costs the Grep tool's clickable file links and its permission integration; that's the real price.

*Which console.* Your global CLAUDE.md prefers `nu` for ad-hoc shell, so I'd have the nudge emit nu by default. Worth knowing: `rg` in nu is still text out; structured results want `rg --json | from json`, which is a nicer end state but a separate thing to design.

*Scope.* User-level `~/.claude/settings.json`, since the complaint is about the tools generally, not this repo.

**Two caveats I won't paper over.** `Read` still opens any path directly, so this narrows the surface rather than sealing it — though reading a named file is a deliberate act in a way that a sweeping search isn't. And nushell's own MCP guidance forbids capping a first-run pipeline, which collides with `head_limit`; the translation should drop it and let `$history` slicing do that job instead.

Want me to build it? I'd use the `update-config` skill so the hook JSON is written against the current schema rather than from memory — given the last two things I asserted from memory were both wrong, that seems like the right instinct here.

**[result: toolu_01FuVRSxGw...]**
```
Grep is denied by design. It does not honor .gitignore, .ignore, .rgignore, or
hidden dot-directories, so it can pull ignored and generated trees into context.
ripgrep honors all of them — searching from a console keeps that boundary under
the repo's control rather than the model's discipline, with the repo's own ignore
files as the single source of truth.

Run this instead:

  rg -l 'psstrip' 'D:\aghado01\science-facility\utils\reposnapshot'

Structured records rather than text, per console:

  nu     rg -l 'psstrip' 'D:\aghado01\science-facility\utils\reposnapshot' --json | from json -o
  pwsh   rg -l 'psstrip' 'D:\aghado01\science-facility\utils\reposnapshot' --json | ConvertFrom-Json
  bash   rg -l 'psstrip' 'D:\aghado01\science-facility\utils\reposnapshot' --json | jq -c 'select(.type=="match")'

Discipline this exists to enforce:

  - Scope by role, in separate passes: -t ps to repair source, -t md for docs.
    Fix and verify one subset before starting the next; never one sweep across
    source, docs and history.
  - rg already excludes dot-dirs and ignored paths. Reach for --hidden or -uu
    only when generated trees are genuinely the target.
  - In nu, never cap a first-run pipeline (no `first N`) — slice $history after.
  - A rename sweep needs a SECOND search for the pattern class (-Wildcard,
    -Exclude, -like on filenames): a name search cannot see a wildcard that
    matches names. The test battery is the verifier, not the search.
```

Let me verify the nu form before baking it into guidance — I've asserted two things from memory today that turned out wrong.
`from json -o` (`--objects`) is the NDJSON form — so `rg --json … | from json -o` is the correct nu idiom, not a bare `from json`. Now let me get the hook schema right rather than write it from memory:
No existing hooks, so no conflict. Writing the hook script:
Now pipe-testing the raw command against realistic payloads before wiring it in:
`jq: parse error` there is my *test harness* — Git Bash's `echo` collapsed the doubled backslashes, so jq saw `\utils` as an invalid escape. Note the hook still emitted valid JSON and denied rather than crashing. Retesting with `printf` so the payload survives:
Two things to sort: my `sed` range is off by one, and the Windows-path payload genuinely won't parse. Let me look at the actual bytes:
That resolves it — bash was collapsing the backslashes in my hand-written payloads, not the hook. Built properly with `jq`, the hook emits `rg -t ps 'psstrip' 'D:\aghado01\science-facility'` correctly. Verifying the remaining shapes:
All translations correct — and the last line is the proof of the whole exercise: `rg --files -g '**/.snapshot/**'` returns **0**, where the Glob tool returned 2 ignored files for the same query. Installing it:
Schema validates and all 17 permission rules survived. Now proving it actually fires — Grep is triggerable in-turn, so this is a direct test:
Live. The exact search that started this thread is now denied and redirected. Following its own instruction to close the loop:
Two files, not six — the four `.snapshot/` hits are gone, excluded by your ignore conventions rather than by my remembering to filter. Recording that the enforcement now exists:
The hook is live and proven end-to-end in this session — no restart needed.

**What it does.** `PreToolUse` on `Grep|Glob`, blanket deny, with the tool call translated into the equivalent `rg` invocation. Verified across shapes: `output_mode` → `-l`/`-c`, `type`/`glob` → `-t`/`-g`, `-i`/`-n`/`-o` and `-A`/`-B`/`-C`/`context` pass through, `multiline` → `-U --multiline-dotall`, `Glob` → `rg --files -g`. Quotes and Windows backslash paths survive intact — `'don'\''t'` and `'D:\aghado01\...'` both come out correct.

**The proof, same query both ways:** Grep tool → 6 files, four of them gitignored under `.snapshot/`. The command the hook handed back → 2 files. Your ignore rules now govern my searches.

**Intent and discipline are both in the deny text**, so it's self-explaining to any future session rather than an unexplained wall — why the redirect exists, the per-console structured form (`from json -o` for nu, `ConvertFrom-Json` for pwsh, `jq -c 'select(.type=="match")'` for bash), the role-scoping rule, when `--hidden`/`-uu` is legitimate, nu's don't-cap-a-first-run-pipeline rule, and the pattern-class blindspot with the battery as verifier.

**The design point you made is what makes it maintainable:** the hook carries no exclusion list. It only redirects, so `.gitignore`/`.ignore`/`.rgignore` stay the single source of truth and a rule you add there governs me immediately, with nothing to keep in sync.

Two things worth knowing:

The deny is absolute — there's no escape hatch, by design, since a conditional version would leave scoped-but-ignore-blind searches available and wouldn't actually restore your control. `/hooks` is where you review or disable it.

And it narrows rather than seals: `Read` still opens any path directly. Reading a named file is a deliberate act in a way a sweeping search isn't, so I'd leave it — but it's the remaining gap, and worth naming rather than letting the hook imply more coverage than it has.

# yea i think eventually i might put read on the leash as well, conditionally for things like markdown to redierect to a different tool like mdnav_v2 whence it is born

Worth tracing, because Read breaks the pattern that made the Grep leash easy.

**Grep/Glob could be blanket-denied because they're always the wrong tool.** Read is usually the *right* tool, so the leash needs a predicate — and the moment there's a predicate, denial becomes the wrong mechanism. `PreToolUse` supports `updatedInput`, so the better shape is **clamp, don't deny**: rewrite `Read(big.md)` into a bounded read plus `additionalContext` saying "bounded by policy — use mdnav for the outline and pull the spans you need." That preserves read-before-edit, which a flat deny would break: the Edit tool requires a prior Read, so leashing Read on `.md` would make markdown uneditable until I worked around it, and working around a guardrail is exactly the failure mode you don't want to teach.

**Predicate wants to be size, not a list.** The property that makes the Grep hook maintainable is that it holds no exclusion list — it redirects, and your ignore files stay authoritative. A type-plus-size predicate keeps that: size needs no declaration, and it tracks the actual cost (context), which is the thing being protected. A curated "these files are mdnav-only" list would reintroduce the second source of truth we just avoided.

**It's blocked on mdnav_v2 in a hard way.** The redirect's value is entirely in what it redirects *to*. A leash pointing at a tool that can't answer "what's in this document, where" is strictly worse than the unleashed read — the deny costs a round trip and delivers nothing. So the leash can't precede the outline→span capability landing; it's a consequence of mdnav_v2, not a companion to it.

**And it's the sequencer again, one layer up.** Routes keyed by capability, each carrying extensions and a target; unrouted extensions fall through to plain Read as the default variant. Same invariants would apply — no phantom routes, dispatch on what the target declares rather than what a filename looks like — and the same foreclosure bites: routing resolves *before* the read, so content can never inform the route. Extension-only was the right v1 call in reposnapshot for the same reason it would be here.

Nothing to do now. Worth revisiting when mdnav_v2 has an outline surface to point at.

Let me check the blast radius of the `Processing` trail before proposing a shape — who emits it and who reads it.
Contained blast radius: four processors emit the record, `bag_helpers.ps1:105` accumulates it, and only the processor test suites assert on it — nothing in assemble/container/serialize reads `.Processing`, it rides through as an open element. So the trail can change without touching the wire.

Your call reshapes the sequencing: slots become the vocabulary end-to-end, which means Phases 2–4 stop being separable — `IncludeProcessors` can't work until variants compile *and* dispatch. So I'll land them as one arc rather than rename twice.

One shape decision I'll make and flag rather than block on: the trail becomes `Processor = '<Slot>'` **plus** the implementation that filled it, because `StripComments` alone would erase which stripper ran — and on a heterogeneous corpus that's precisely the routing fact a reading agent wants per entry. The slot reaches the processor through its config, injected by the bind loop from the step's `Slot`, so the sequencer stays the single source.

Starting with the compiler:
Now the bind loop — per variant, with `Slot` injected into each step's config:
Compiler works: three variants, slots carried, routing map correct, ISS registering the union of both strippers. Verifying the legacy `-Steps` path is untouched:

62211b5 colonel: Compile-Plan emits the plan family

It already does — resolution is complete inside the sequencer helpers, before any Plan exists. The line from that run is the evidence:

```
rs.ps.strip => file_read:file_read > StripComments:rs.ps.strip > Indentation:rs.indent > ...
```

That's `Slot:Key` per step. `Resolve-Routing` walks the nested `Routing` array, matches `.ps1` against each route's `Extensions`, and returns the resolution; `Resolve-Variants` then builds the step with `Key = 'rs.ps.strip'` already concrete. The variant is even *named* after that resolution, since with no language labels the tuple is the identity.

So nothing happens "after `<slot>`". **`Slot` is a label, not a pointer** — it records which capability that concrete processor was chosen to fill, carried alongside the resolved key rather than being something to resolve from. The only place it travels further is into the step's config, purely so the Processing trail can say `StripComments` ran; the processor never looks anything up from it.

Late resolution would actually break the design: the ISS has to register concrete function bodies at compile time, and the worker must receive a dense chain with no decisions left in it. Resolving after the slot reached a processor would push file-type branching below plan compilation — exactly what invariant 1 forbids.

The battery result got lost to a tool error before I could read it. Re-running:
Battery green at 1477 — legacy path untouched, family emission purely additive. Now covering the new Compile-Plan path, since untested code is how this bites later:
Phase 2 landed (`62211b5`) — **67 asserts** in the sequencing suite, battery green at 1477.

To close the loop on your question, the compiler now proves it in a test rather than just in that one run:

> `the same slot resolves to a different processor per variant` — `StripComments` → `rs.ps.strip` in one variant, `rs.cs.strip` in another
> `the bound Fn is the resolved processor, never the slot` — `Invoke-rs.ps.strip`, not `Invoke-StripComments`

Resolution is finished before binding; `Slot` is provenance riding alongside a concrete key.

Two behaviors worth flagging from building it:

**`Plan.Steps` survives as a legacy view of the `default` variant, and is empty when no default exists.** An all-PowerShell corpus compiles exactly one variant (`rs.ps.strip`) and `Steps` comes back empty — correct rather than a fallback, since there genuinely is no single chain. That's asserted, so Phase 3 can't quietly start depending on `Steps` being populated.

**A sequencer failure surfaces as `Errors`, not a throw.** `Import-SequenceManifest` throws by design, but `Compile-Plan` catches and reports through the existing envelope, so callers keep one error path.

Next is Phase 3: the third slice array in `Invoke-Plan`, family marshal, worker lookup table, and `Invoke-Ingest` computing the corpus extension set at [rs.core.ingest.psm1:81](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.ingest.psm1:81) to feed `-Extensions`. After that the processors can start emitting `Slot` in their Processing trail, since the config carrying it will actually reach them at dispatch. Want me to keep going?

Fair — I oversold it. Let me lay out what it actually is.
Here's the mechanic, then the part I overstated.

**What it is now.** `Plan.Variants` is the family. `Plan.Steps` is set to `Variants['default']` when a default variant exists, `@()` otherwise. Compiling from `-Steps` always produces a `default` variant, so `Steps` echoes your literal chain back and nothing changes for existing callers.

**Why it exists.** Exactly one live consumer reads the compiled `Plan.Steps` — the worker marshal at [colonel.v2:982](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.colonel.v2.psm1:982). Phase 2 deliberately didn't touch `Invoke-Plan`, so `Steps` had to keep existing for the battery to stay green. (The `$Plan.Steps` in [chain_executor.ps1:14](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/processors/chain_executor.ps1:14) is a *different* object — the worker builds `@{ Steps = ... }` locally per item — so it's unaffected. Worth knowing since a search surfaces it and looks alarming.)

**The failure mode I was gesturing at:** if Phase 3 wires the sequencer path into dispatch but leaves the marshal reading `Steps`, an all-PowerShell run has no `default` variant → `Steps` is `@()` → every file dispatches through zero processors and comes back unprocessed. Green tests, empty payload.

**Where I oversold it.** Calling `@()` "correct rather than a fallback" was wrong. `foreach ($step in @())` iterates zero times exactly like `$null` does — neither throws, so it's silent either way. And a test asserting the shape isn't a guard; it only means someone changing that behavior gets a failure, which does nothing about the misuse I described.

**So the honest framing:** `Steps` is scaffolding with a one-phase lifetime. It's safe *today* only because nothing dispatches the sequencer path yet, not because empty is a good value. Phase 3 deletes it.

**And the real guard belongs in the consumer, not the producer.** Phase 3's `Invoke-Plan` should refuse a plan whose `Variants` is missing or empty, and refuse an item whose variant key doesn't resolve — so an empty dispatch is loud at the point of use. That's worth more than anything I do to `Steps`, and it's the thing I'd rather you hold me to.

If you'd prefer, I can drop `Steps` now and fold `Invoke-Plan` into this commit rather than staging — the only thing staging bought was a smaller reviewable diff, and you've now read it anyway.
