You're right, and the distinction I blurred makes it clearer: **`-Steps` the input and `Plan.Steps` the output are separate questions.**

The input must survive — it's `-RunVerbatim`'s mechanism and several suites drive it. The *output* field has exactly one consumer, the marshal at :982, which Phase 3 rewrites anyway. So preserving it buys one commit's green battery and nothing else — while creating a derived duplicate of one family member that can disagree with its source. That's the same "one fact, two places" defect I spent this afternoon removing from the contract dispatch. Counterproductive.

Folding Phase 3 in now.
Now the marshal and worker — the family shared by reference, plan selection as a dictionary lookup:
Now dropping `Plan.Steps` entirely and having Ingest feed the corpus extensions:
Battery green at **1496** with `Plan.Steps` gone entirely — `Variants` is now the only representation, and every existing `-Steps` caller works through the family-of-one path.
Phase 3 landed (`e1cb6ce`). Battery green at 1496.

You were right that preserving it was compounding complexity. Deleting `Plan.Steps` turned out to *shrink* the diff — the marshal was going to be rewritten either way, and keeping a legacy view meant maintaining a second representation that could drift from `Variants`.

**What replaced the scaffolding is a real guard, in the consumer.** `Invoke-Plan` now refuses a plan with no variants outright, and an item whose variant key doesn't resolve is an error naming the extension. That's the loudness the `@()` never provided — an empty dispatch returning unprocessed content with no failure anywhere is exactly the silent-wrong worth spending code on.

**Still to do, and the reason I'm pausing rather than continuing:** the per-item routing isn't *proven* yet. The battery is green because every existing caller drives `-Steps`, which routes everything to `default` — the family-of-one path. Nothing yet dispatches a heterogeneous corpus and checks that a `.ps1` got `rs.ps.strip` while a `.cs` got `rs.cs.strip`. That test is next, and `tests/languages/` is exactly the fixture for it.

**One decision I want from you rather than guessing**, since it's payload shape and I've guessed wrong twice today. The Processing record currently reads `Processor = 'rs.ps.strip'`. Two ways to bring slots in:

- **Add `Slot` alongside** — `Processor: rs.ps.strip, Slot: StripComments`. Each field means one thing regardless of how the processor was invoked, and direct unit-test invocations (no sequencer, no slot) simply omit it.
- **Make `Processor` the slot** and move the stub to a second field. Reads better for an agent consuming the payload, but the headline field then means "capability" in a dispatched run and "implementation" when a test calls the processor directly — polymorphic by invocation context.

I lean to the first for the non-polymorphism, but you framed this as wanting the trail to speak capabilities, so it's your call which field carries the name.

---

# im glad you noticed the hazard with test-driven planning. which is counter-factual reasoning about existing tests and the imperative to keep them green versus building the new system design and writing new tests. doing surgical changes like this is going to break things and we can't be intimidated by tests written for the old way when we are trying to pave the new way. this is a judgement call, i do appreciate the point of test-driven development, but i think my point is worth considering here

Your point lands, and the sharpest evidence is in my own reasoning from ten minutes ago — not just in `Plan.Steps`.

I argued for `Slot` alongside `Processor` on "non-polymorphism" grounds: the headline field shouldn't mean *capability* in a dispatched run and *implementation* when a test calls the processor directly. That sounds principled. But the payload is only ever produced by a real dispatched run, where the slot is always known. The ambiguity I was protecting against **exists only in a context that never ships** — a unit test invoking a processor in a shape production no longer produces. I reached that argument by reasoning from the suite rather than from the deliverable, which is precisely the hazard you're naming, dressed up as a design principle.

So I'm reversing: `Processor` carries the slot. And those unit tests aren't merely permitted to change — they're mildly *wrong*, because they exercise an invocation shape the new design doesn't generate. Passing a `Slot` in their config makes them more faithful, not less.

The criterion I'd use to tell the two apart: **does the test encode a behavior the system promises, or a shape the old implementation happened to have?** `chain: two records accumulated in order` is a promise — keep it, let it constrain. `Processing record names the processor`, where "the processor" means the file stub, is implementation shape — change it. **[result: toolu_01KTBHqBTQ...]**
```
934bb14 colonel: the variant key is an identity; the Plan carries what it means
```

You've put your finger on a real ambiguity, and it's worth naming that `rs.ps.strip` currently appears in **three** distinct roles:

| Role | Where | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| Implementation | `Implementation` on the trail, step `Key`, `configs/<key>.json`, `Invoke-<key>` | which file ran |
| **Variant key** | `Plan.Variants` keys, `Plan.Routing` values | **which chain this class of file gets** |
| Manifest key | discovery from disk | what exists |

The chokepoint you're describing is the middle one, and here's the thing that resolves the confusion: **the variant key spelling `rs.ps.strip` is an accident of there being exactly one routed slot.** With a second slot it becomes a compound, and the coincidence vanishes. So the key is an *identity*, not a name — nothing should ever parse it, match on it, or show it to anyone as if it named a processor. That it's internal is precisely what makes it safe to be descriptive; it buys debuggability with no contract cost.

Two mechanical consequences worth fixing now rather than discovering later.

**The Plan should carry the resolutions.** `Resolve-Routing` already computes variantKey → {slot → implementation}, but I dropped it after building the family. Without it, Phase 5's echo would have to reverse-engineer meaning by splitting the key string — which is exactly the parsing I just said must never happen. Carrying it makes the echo read resolutions directly and stays legible at N slots.

**The join separator should be one that cannot occur in a processor key.** I used `+`, which is legal in filenames — so two distinct tuples could in principle join to the same string and silently share a compiled chain. `|` is illegal in Windows filenames, making that collision structurally impossible rather than merely unlikely.
Committed (`934bb14`). Battery 1514.

To answer the "how does this play forward" directly — the rule that makes it tractable is **the variant key is an identity, the Plan's `Resolutions` is the meaning.** Everything follows from that:

- **Today**, a key spells `rs.ps.strip` because one slot is routed. That's a coincidence, not a design fact, so no code should encode it.
- **With a second routed slot**, keys become compounds and every consumer keeps working, because none of them reads the key for meaning — `Invoke-Plan` does a dictionary lookup, the echo reads `Resolutions`.
- **Debuggability survives** because the key stays descriptive. An error naming `rs.ps.strip|md.parse` tells you the resolution at a glance, which an ordinal like `v2` wouldn't.

The two roles you were worried about colliding — implementation name vs. chain identity — now stay separable *because* nothing has to distinguish them by inspection. The step's `Key` is the implementation; the family's key is the identity; `Resolutions` maps between them explicitly.

On "it's entirely internal, so it's OK to carry the specific name" — agreed, and the reason is worth keeping: internal is what makes a descriptive key *safe*. It never reaches the payload, so no consumer can grow a dependency on its spelling. The moment it surfaced in a wire column or a manifest field, it would stop being an identity and become a name someone parses.

The two asserts I added pin exactly that: `Resolutions['rs.ps.strip']['StripComments'] -eq 'rs.ps.strip'` reads meaning without splitting a string, and `Resolutions['default'].Count -eq 0` says the default variant resolved nothing rather than resolving to something empty.

Phase 4 next whenever you want it — that's the one that makes `./rs.core.user.ps1` actually route on a mixed corpus, and it's the last piece before the echo has something real to report.

**[result: toolu_012ibn3oAo...]**

c37ab78 colonel: the resolution is an ordered list; the variant key is derived from it
```

Your instinct found a real defect, not just a stylistic one. Look at what `Resolve-Routing` actually does:

```powershell
$hits[$slot] = $route[0].Key      # unordered hashtable
$parts.Add($route[0].Key)          # ordered list  ← built, joined, then DISCARDED
```

The ordered list already exists. I join it to make the key and then throw it away, keeping only `$hits` — a plain hashtable, which in PowerShell has **no guaranteed iteration order**. So the artifact that survives has lost the ordering, and the string is the only place canon order is encoded. Invisible today with one routed slot; wrong the moment there are two and the echo tries to report them in order.

So the answer to "why join a list to a string only to make it a list again" is: we shouldn't, and the fix isn't to defend the join — it's to keep the list as the artifact and let the key be *derived* from it.
Committed (`c37ab78`). Battery 1514.

To close out the mechanism question directly, since there were two things tangled in it:

**The defect your instinct found** was real and now fixed: `Resolutions` is an ordered array of `(Slot, Key)` pairs in canon order, and `Resolve-Variants` matches on that list rather than a keyed map. The list is operated on as a list everywhere it's used — no reconstruction anywhere.

**The join itself survives, but demoted.** It's now explicitly a *canonical form derived from* the list, existing for one reason: a hashtable key needs value equality, and a PowerShell array as a key hashes by reference — two structurally identical arrays would be two different keys, which would defeat the dedup that makes `.ps1`/`.psm1`/`.psd1` share one compiled chain. So the derivation is canonicalization-for-hashing, not serialization. Crucially, nothing ever splits it back — that's what makes it legitimate rather than a round-trip.

The alternative I considered and rejected: an opaque ordinal key (`v0`, `v1`) with structural comparison for dedup. It removes the separator question entirely and makes parsing unthinkable, at the cost of `Variants['v1']` in tests and error messages instead of `Variants['rs.ps.strip']`. At this scale the dedup scan would be free, so it's purely a legibility trade — and with the list now authoritative, the key carries no meaning anyone needs, so the descriptive spelling costs nothing and helps when something goes wrong. Worth knowing the option exists if the compound keys ever get unwieldy.

That also retires the thing that prompted this: the separator choice is no longer load-bearing for *meaning*, only for collision-freedom in the derived key, which `|` gives structurally since it can't appear in a Windows filename.

**[result: toolu_01NtC6ugkK...]**
```

c37ab78 colonel: the resolution is an ordered list; the variant key is derived from it
934bb14 colonel: the variant key is an identity; the Plan carries what it means
```

Because I over-corrected. You pointed at order loss in a hashtable, I swung to a list of pair-objects — and in doing so gave up the keyed lookup, which is why `Resolve-Variants` now does `Where-Object { $_.Slot -eq $slot }`, a pipeline scan to answer a question a dictionary answers directly.

The access pattern is *both* sequential and keyed: the echo walks slots in canon order, `Resolve-Variants` looks one up by name. A list gives the first and forces a scan for the second; a plain hashtable gives the second and loses the first. `[ordered]@{}` — `OrderedDictionary` — gives both natively, and it's the shape the requirement actually has.
Committed (`03a18a2`). Battery 1516, sequencing at 87.

The structure landed where it should have gone directly:

```
Resolutions[variantKey]  →  [ordered] StripComments = rs.ps.strip
```

Walked in order for reporting, indexed by slot for compilation — `$pairs.Contains($slot)` / `$pairs[$slot]` instead of a pipeline scan. Three asserts pin it: keyed by slot in canon order, direct lookup, and the type is actually `OrderedDictionary` so ordering isn't accidental.

Worth naming the pattern in those three commits, since it's the same mistake twice: I had a hashtable, you flagged the lost order, I swung to a list and lost the keyed access, you flagged the scan. Each correction fixed the named defect and introduced its mirror image, because I was reacting to the last symptom instead of asking what the access pattern actually was. Reading it forward — *walked in order by one consumer, keyed by another* — names `OrderedDictionary` immediately and skips both intermediate states.

The variant key survives unchanged in role: still a derived canonical form, needed because `OrderedDictionary` hashes by reference, so two structurally-equal resolutions would otherwise compile as two variants instead of sharing one. That's the whole job it does now.

Where things stand: phases 1–3 done and proven end to end, the trail speaks capabilities, the resolution artifact is right. Phase 4 — `-IncludeProcessors` at [rs.core.user.ps1:101](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:101), the config key at :175, deleting the hand-built steps block, `-RunVerbatim`, splitting the cautions — is what makes `./rs.core.user.ps1` route for real on a mixed corpus.

1	The occupancy test is the algorithm. The hair is that `Resolve-Routing` answers occupancy, then `Resolve-Variants` **joins that answer back onto the spine**.
2	
3	What you have now:
4	
5	1. For each unique extension, filter `Routes` and collect `(Slot, Key)` pairs.
6	2. Name the variant by joining those keys with `|`.
7	3. Walk `Enabled` again and `$pairs | Where-Object { $_.Slot -eq $slot }` to rebuild the chain.
8	
9	Step 3 exists only because step 1 threw the spine away. The comment that nothing may parse the `|` key is the tell: internment was encoded as a string join, then you had to warn yourself not to treat it as data.
10	
11	The walk you had in mind is one pass. Sort the enabled set once, occupancy-test each routed slot, splice on miss:
12	
13	```powershell
14	function Resolve-Chain($Sequence, $Enabled, [string]$Extension)
15	{
16	    $ext = '.' + $Extension.TrimStart('.').ToLowerInvariant()
17	    $steps = foreach ($slot in ($Enabled | Sort-Object { $Sequence.Processors[$_].Group }, { $Sequence.Processors[$_].Rank }))
18	    {
19	        $meta = $Sequence.Processors[$slot]
20	        if ($meta.IsRouted)
21	        {
22	            $hit = @($meta.Routes | Where-Object { $ext -in $_.Extensions })
23	            if ($hit.Count -eq 0) { continue }          # splice
24	            $key = $hit[0].Key
25	        }
26	        else { $key = $meta.Key }
27	
28	        [pscustomobject]@{ Key = $key; Slot = $slot; Config = @{} }
29	    }
30	    @($steps)
31	}
32	```
33	
34	That is the helper: `$ext -in $_.Extensions` on the JSON records. No pair list, no `|` identity, no second join.
35	
36	Internment stays, but it is a **cache over unique extensions**, not a relation:
37	
38	```powershell
39	$variants = @{}
40	$extMap = @{}
41	foreach ($ext in $uniqueExts)
42	{
43	    $chain = Resolve-Chain $seq $enabled $ext
44	    $id = [string]($variants.Keys | Where-Object { -not (Compare-Object $variants[$_].Key $chain.Key) } | Select-Object -First 1)
45	    if (-not $id)
46	    {
47	        $id = [guid]::NewGuid().ToString('N')   # opaque; dispatch never prints it
48	        $variants[$id] = $chain
49	    }
50	    $extMap[$ext] = $id
51	}
52	```
53	
54	`.ps1` and `.psm1` still share a chain because `Compare-Object` on the Key lists agrees, not because both stringify to `rs.ps.strip`. Dispatch stays `Extension → intern id → frozen steps`. Workers never walk Routing.
55	
56	`Resolve-Routing` + `Resolve-Variants` collapse into `Resolve-Chain` plus that intern loop. Compile still runs it over the corpus extension set ingest already collected — not per file. Per-item work is the dictionary lookup you already have in `Invoke-Plan`.
57	
58	The `|` keys in the tests (`ExtensionMap['.ps1'] -eq 'rs.ps.strip'`) are asserting the intern label, not the chain. After the collapse, assert the chain (`file_read > rs.ps.strip > …`) and that sibling extensions point at the same intern id. The label itself is not a fact.
```

```json
{"command":"\u0022default:      \u0022 \u002B $(if (Compare-Object @(\u0027a\u0027,\u0027b\u0027) @(\u0027b\u0027,\u0027a\u0027)) { \u0027differs\u0027 } else { \u0027EQUAL\u0027 })\n\u0022syncwindow 0: \u0022 \u002B $(if (Compare-Object @(\u0027a\u0027,\u0027b\u0027) @(\u0027b\u0027,\u0027a\u0027) -SyncWindow 0) { \u0027differs\u0027 } else { \u0027EQUAL\u0027 })\n\u0022len mismatch: \u0022 \u002B $(if (Compare-Object @(\u0027a\u0027,\u0027b\u0027) @(\u0027a\u0027)) { \u0027differs\u0027 } else ... [truncated]

**[result: toolu_01XJhAhZ3Y...]**
```
default:      EQUAL
syncwindow 0: differs
len mismatch: differs
```

Not superseded — it supersedes mine, including the last three commits.

**The critique is correct on its central point.** `Resolve-Routing` answers occupancy, then `Resolve-Variants` walks the spine *again* to re-join that answer onto the ordering. The pair list exists only to carry an answer between two passes over the same structure. `Resolve-Chain` tests occupancy at the slot where it matters, in the single walk that's already producing ordered steps, so the intermediate has nothing to carry.

**And it kills something bigger than the join: `Resolutions` shouldn't exist at all.** Every bound step already carries `Slot` alongside `Key`. So `Plan.Variants[id]` fully describes what resolved to what, in canon order, and the echo reads slots straight off the chain. The parallel map I've been maintaining duplicates information the steps already hold — which means the hashtable → list → OrderedDictionary arc you walked me through was refining a structure that should have been deleted. Your "why join a list to a string" instinct was pointing at that; I kept fixing the artifact instead of asking whether it was needed.

**Two corrections before adopting it, though.**

The `[guid]::NewGuid()` intern id is wrong for this system specifically — it makes the Plan non-deterministic run to run, and determinism is load-bearing here (selfie asserts *"the plan is deterministic from one IR"*). An **ordinal** is equally opaque, equally unprintable, and stable across runs on the same corpus.

And `Compare-Object` defaults to **set** semantics, not positional — just verified:

```
Compare-Object @('a','b') @('b','a')   →  EQUAL
Compare-Object @('a','b') @('b','a') -SyncWindow 0  →  differs
```

So two chains with the same processors in a different order would intern as one. That's safe *only* because the canon fixes order — i.e. it leans on an invariant maintained in another function. Given how much of today was spent removing exactly that kind of implicit coupling, I'd compare positionally rather than inherit it.

**One refinement:** `Resolve-Chain` sorts the enabled set on every call, so it re-sorts per extension. Hoist the sort out and pass the ordered slot list in — the canon doesn't change between extensions.

**Test consequences it names correctly.** `ExtensionMap['.ps1'] -eq 'rs.ps.strip'` asserts the label, not a fact; under the collapse it becomes "sibling extensions share one id" plus "that id's chain is `file_read > rs.ps.strip > …`". My section 4 assert *"variant key is the resolution itself"* stops being a property and becomes an implementation detail — it should go rather than be updated.

Want me to do the collapse? It's a net deletion: two functions become one, `Resolutions` disappears from the Plan, the separator question evaporates, and the "nothing may parse this" comment goes with it — which was, as the doc says, the tell that something was off.

```
390	function Resolve-Routing
391	{
392	    <#
393	    .SYNOPSIS
394	        Maps each corpus extension to the variant that will process it.
395	
396	    .DESCRIPTION
397	        The sequencer names no languages, so a file class IS its resolution tuple:
398	        for each enabled routed slot, which route claims the extension. Extensions
399	        that resolve identically therefore share one variant by construction, and an
400	        extension no enabled route claims lands on 'default'. A variant is compiled
401	        only if some corpus extension actually resolves to it.
402	
403	    .OUTPUTS
404	        [PSCustomObject] @{ ExtensionMap; Resolutions }
405	          ExtensionMap — extension -> variant key
406	          Resolutions  — variant key -> [ordered] slot -> implementation, in canon
407	                         order. This map is the artifact; the key is a canonical
408	                         form derived from it, for lookup and dedup only.
409	    #>
410	    param(
411	        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [pscustomobject]           $Sequence,
412	        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [string[]]                 $Enabled,
413	        [AllowEmptyCollection()] [AllowNull()] [string[]] $Extensions = @()
414	    )
415	
416	    $procs = $Sequence.Processors
417	
418	    $routedSlots = @(
419	        @($Enabled) |
420	            Where-Object { $procs[$_].IsRouted } |
421	            Sort-Object { $procs[$_].Group }, { $procs[$_].Rank }
422	    )
423	
424	    $extMap = @{}
425	    $resolutions = @{}
426	
427	    foreach ($x in @($Extensions))
428	    {
429	        if ([string]::IsNullOrWhiteSpace($x)) { continue }
430	        $ext = '.' + ([string]$x).TrimStart('.').ToLowerInvariant()
431	        if ($extMap.ContainsKey($ext)) { continue }
432	
433	        # The resolution is an ORDERED MAP of slot -> implementation. It is read both
434	        # ways — walked in canon order by the echo, and looked up by slot when
435	        # compiling a variant — so it is an OrderedDictionary rather than a list
436	        # (which would force a scan) or a hashtable (which would lose the order).
437	        # $routedSlots is already sorted by (Group, Rank), so insertion is the canon.
438	        $pairs = [ordered]@{}
439	
440	        foreach ($slot in $routedSlots)
441	        {
442	            # Occupancy is decided on the route records themselves; the flattened
443	            # union only ever answers "claimed at all", which falls out of $pairs.
444	            $route = @($procs[$slot].Routes | Where-Object { $ext -in $_.Extensions })
445	            if ($route.Count -eq 0) { continue }
446	            $pairs[$slot] = $route[0].Key
447	        }
448	
449	        # The key is an IDENTITY DERIVED from that map — a canonical form for
450	        # dictionary lookup and dedup, never the artifact itself, because an
451	        # OrderedDictionary hashes by reference and two equal resolutions must be one
452	        # variant. Nothing may parse it, match on it, or display it as if it named a
453	        # processor; read the pairs. The separator is illegal in a filename, so no two
454	        # distinct resolutions collapse onto one key.
455	        $variantKey = 'default'
456	        if ($pairs.Count -gt 0) { $variantKey = (@($pairs.Values) -join '|') }
457	
458	        $extMap[$ext] = $variantKey
459	        if (-not $resolutions.ContainsKey($variantKey)) { $resolutions[$variantKey] = $pairs }
460	    }
461	
462	    return [pscustomobject]@{
463	        ExtensionMap = $extMap
464	        Resolutions  = $resolutions
465	    }
466	}
467	
468	function Resolve-Variants
469	{
470	    <#
471	    .SYNOPSIS
472	        Compiles one dense step list per variant.
473	
474	    .DESCRIPTION
475	        Each variant walks the enabled set, takes its own resolution for every routed
476	        slot, and splices out any slot that did not resolve — variants differ in
477	        length and carry no holes. Sorting is by (Group, Rank).
478	
479	    .PARAMETER Resolutions
480	        Variant key -> ordered slot -> implementation map, from Resolve-Routing.
481	
482	    .OUTPUTS
483	        [hashtable] variant key -> [PSCustomObject[]] @{ Key; Slot; Config }
484	    #>
485	    param(
486	        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [pscustomobject] $Sequence,
487	        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [string[]]       $Enabled,
488	        [Parameter(Mandatory)] [hashtable]      $Resolutions
489	    )
490	
491	    $procs = $Sequence.Processors
492	    $variants = @{}
493	
494	    foreach ($variantKey in $Resolutions.Keys)
495	    {
496	        $pairs = $Resolutions[$variantKey]
497	        $steps = [System.Collections.Generic.List[object]]::new()
498	
499	        foreach ($slot in @($Enabled))
500	        {
501	            $meta = $procs[$slot]
502	
503	            if ($meta.IsRouted)
504	            {
505	                # A routed slot this variant did not resolve is spliced out entirely.
506	                if (-not $pairs.Contains($slot)) { continue }
507	                $resolved = [string]$pairs[$slot]
508	            }
509	            else { $resolved = $meta.Key }
510	
511	            $steps.Add([pscustomobject]@{
512	                    Key   = $resolved
513	                    Slot  = $slot
514	                    Group = $meta.Group
515	                    Rank  = $meta.Rank
516	                })
517	        }
518	
519	        $variants[$variantKey] = @(
520	            $steps | Sort-Object Group, Rank | ForEach-Object {
521	                [pscustomobject]@{ Key = $_.Key; Slot = $_.Slot; Config = @{} }
522	            }
523	        )
524	    }
525	
526	    return $variants
527	}

**[result: toolu_01UUWCRRky...]**
```
aa146c5 colonel: collapse routing and variant-building into one walk of the canon
```

This is a 138-line replacement of two whole functions, so let me read the exact span rather than reconstruct it:
Now wiring Compile-Plan and Invoke-Plan to the collapsed shape:
Now section 7, which asserted labels rather than facts:
Done (`aa146c5`). Battery **1518**, sequencing **89**.

**What I took from the brief.** The central diagnosis was right: two passes over the same spine, with a pair map existing only to carry an answer between them. `Resolve-Chain` tests occupancy at the slot where it matters, inside the pass already emitting ordered steps — so there's no intermediate and no identity to derive. Interning became a cache over unique extensions, which retired the derived key, the separator, *and* the "nothing may parse this" comment that the brief correctly read as the tell.

The bigger win it surfaced indirectly: **`Resolutions` is gone entirely.** Every step already carries `Slot` beside `Key`, so the chain *is* the resolution. That means the hashtable → list → OrderedDictionary arc was polishing a structure that shouldn't have existed — your "why join a list to a string" instinct was pointing at that the whole time.

**What I didn't take.**

The `[guid]::NewGuid()` intern id would make the Plan differ run to run. Determinism is asserted elsewhere in this codebase (*"the plan is deterministic from one IR"*), so ids are **ordinals** — equally opaque, equally unprintable, stable across runs. There's now a test that the same corpus interns identically regardless of the order extensions arrive in.

And `Compare-Object` for chain identity defaults to **set** semantics — verified: `@('a','b')` vs `@('b','a')` compares EQUAL. Two chains differing only in order would intern as one. Safe only because the canon fixes order, which is exactly the "invariant maintained in another function" coupling this design keeps deleting. `Test-SameChain` compares positionally, on both `Key` and `Slot`.

One thing I added that the brief didn't address: with opaque ids there's no `'default'` string to fall back to, so the Plan carries `DefaultVariant` — the chain an unrouted file takes, `null` when no corpus extension landed there. That preserves the loud failure we built in Phase 3 rather than letting an unroutable item silently take some chain.

Net: two functions became one plus a small intern loop, one Plan field disappeared, and the test suite grew because it now asserts chains and shared identity instead of labels.

**[result: toolu_011fbXHckh...]**
```
2db5f2a colonel: pass-through is a chain, not a failure
```

That's the right frame, and testing it against my implementation exposes a real gap — not in the splicing, which is correct, but in the fallback I pruned.
Confirmed — a real pass-through failure, and the cause is mine: I pruned the unrouted chain as "unused," but that chain *is* pass-through. An extensionless file (`Makefile`, `LICENSE`) in a PowerShell corpus errors instead of taking the N-1 chain.
Now the tests that asserted the pruning I'm removing:
Now a dispatch test for the case that was failing — an extensionless file in an otherwise-routed corpus:
Fixed and committed (`2db5f2a`). Battery **1523**, sequencing **94**.

Your framing was the diagnosis. The splicing was always right — `.py` and `.ts` correctly got N-1 chains. What was wrong was that I'd pruned the unrouted chain whenever every *corpus* extension happened to be routed, calling it an unused variant. It isn't unused: it **is** pass-through. So a PowerShell corpus containing a `Makefile` or a `LICENSE` compiled no chain for them, and dispatch reported

```
No compiled chain covers item 1 (extension '') — the plan does not cover this corpus.
```

instead of running the N-1 canon. Measured before the fix, now section 10.

Pass-through is compiled first and always, interned like any other chain so an extension resolving nothing simply shares it. `DefaultVariant` is never null on a sequencer plan, and the per-item guard reverts to what it should have been — defence against a malformed plan, not the handler for an expected case.

The part worth flagging: **the pruning had a test asserting it.** *"an all-PowerShell corpus compiles no default variant"* — written by me, early, when I mistook an optimization for a property. It passed all afternoon while encoding the bug. That's the hazard you named earlier arriving from the other direction: not old tests constraining new design, but a *new* test freezing a wrong assumption before the design was understood. Green the whole time, and the battery would never have caught it — only the question "what happens to a file with no extension?" did.

Also worth noting the guard I added in Phase 3 did its job here. It turned what would otherwise have been a silently-unprocessed file into a named error, which is how the probe found it in one shot.

# ok now youa re really concerning me with confabulation. N as i referenced it is the number of steps including a stripping processor step resolved in a chain. Let me show you the actual state rather than describe it — that's the right answer to a confabulation concern.
The slip was real and specific: I wrote **"the N-1 canon."** The canon is the declared stage sequence in the sequencer; a chain is a resolved dense instance of it. Merging those two is exactly the category error worth catching, and your N / N-1 usage is what I'd been using elsewhere — N being a chain that resolved a routed processor, N-1 one that didn't.

The output above is the design as you stated it, verified rather than described:

- **Planning resolves all distinct chains for the ingestion set** — 6 extensions, 3 chains.
- **The set is very small** — and stays small, because chains are interned by structural equality, so `.ps1`/`.psm1` share chain[2] and `.md`/`.py`/`.ts` share chain[0].
- **N and N-1** — chain[1] and chain[2] are length 5 with a stripper; chain[0] is length 4, the same canon with the routed step absent.
- **Assignment is resolved during planning** — the extension→chain map is computed once at compile time, not per file.
- **Dispatch is per item** — each item's chain is a dictionary lookup at slice time, and the worker hands `Invoke-ChainExecutor` that one chain.
- **chain-executor is unchanged** — it still just walks the steps it's handed. What changed is that the steps differ per item instead of being one monolithic list for the whole run.

Vocabulary I'll hold to from here: *canon* is the spine, *chain* is the resolved instance, *N-1* means a chain where routing resolved nothing for a slot, *distinct chains* is the compiled set, *assignment* is which chain an item gets.

Verified against the file rather than memory: [rs.core.user.ps1](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1) is **completely untouched** by the sequencer work. It still declares `-Processors` as a literal ordered array, still reads the config key `'Processors'`, still hand-builds `$steps`, still passes `-Steps` to ingest. So everything built today is unreachable from the CLI.

**Phase 4 — caller surface.** The only thing between the sequencer and real runs:

| Line | Change |
|---|---|
| [:101](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:101) | `-Processors` → `-IncludeProcessors`; add `-RunVerbatim` |
| [:175](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:175) | config key still `'Processors'` — why your `IncludeProcessors` is inert |
| [:228-245](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:228) | delete the hand-built steps block (the `file_read` prepend, the explicit/default branches) |
| [:248-258](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:248) | three cautions → compiler guarantees under the sequencer, retained under `-RunVerbatim` |
| [:261](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:261) | swap `-Steps` for the sequencer params |
| :18, :59, :82, :85 | docstring and examples still describe the ordered-array surface |

Plus `user-config.json`'s `$comment`, which still says "Processors defaults defer to configs/<Key>.json".

**Phase 5 — echo.** `ConfigEcho` at [:272](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:272) reports one chain. It becomes: the distinct chains, and which extensions took each. Straightforward now that the Plan carries `Variants`, `Routing`, and `DefaultVariant` — no reverse-engineering needed.

**Docs — one decision still yours.** [docs/spine-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/spine-and-routing.md) is doubly superseded (Spine array, Languages section, `$`-sigil tokens, `registry.json`); archive or rewrite is still open from before the hooks digression. Your [issues/…/sequencing-routing.md](issues/reposnapshot/design/sequencing-routing.md) describes the pre-slot format — the algorithm holds, the shapes don't. And the cursor discussion is now acted on, worth a note recording the two departures (ordinals not guids, positional comparison not `Compare-Object`) so nobody re-derives them.

Phase 4 is a contained change and it's what makes `./rs.core.user.ps1` route for real. Want it?

Starting with the caller surface. Let me read the spans I'll change:
Now the core replacement — the hand-built chain gives way to the sequencer, with the cautions scoped to verbatim mode:
The echo needs the compiled plan, which ingest doesn't currently surface:
Now the echo — reporting the distinct chains and which extensions took each:
Runs. Now verifying it actually routed rather than silently doing the old thing:
`ConfigEcho` is constructed but never consumed downstream, so nothing broke — but that also means I can't read the routing from it. Better evidence: the old inert path ran `file_read → rs.whitespace → rs.content_meta` with **no stripper**. If the sequencer is live, comments are now gone from the payload:
Let me confirm that's stripping rather than the file simply being absent — checking a *code* string from the same file:
`Test-SameChain` is absent too — so that file isn't in the payload at all, which invalidates my inference. Let me see what actually got ingested:
The file *is* there (row 01). My `-c` returned 0 because rg was treating those payload files as binary. Redoing with `-a`:
Clean proof: code survives, comments and docstrings are gone. Under the old inert path no stripper ran at all, so `StripComments` is genuinely live on the CLI now. Battery:
Two different things. Let me look at the user test first:
Both use the old surface to test a behavioral promise — the promise stays, the surface changes:
Section 6 gets 0 entries — I dropped the unconditional `file_read` prepend, so a verbatim chain never reads any content and silently produces nothing. That's a footgun, not verbatim purity:
Phase 4 and 5 landed (`a263478`). Battery **1527**. Now the doc — let me see what the archive left behind:
Both done. Battery **1527 green**, and the whole arc is now reachable from the CLI.

**Caller surface** (`a263478`). The key fact: [:175](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/rs.core.user.ps1:175) read the config key `'Processors'`, so your `IncludeProcessors` was inert and every run silently took the old default chain — whitespace and content_meta, no stripper at all. Verified on the live corpus after the change: `Test-SameChain` survives in the payload, while `.SYNOPSIS` and comment text don't. The old path couldn't have produced that.

Two things the tests caught that I'd have shipped otherwise:

**`file_read` in verbatim mode.** I removed the unconditional prepend on the grounds that "verbatim means verbatim" — and section 6 immediately returned **0 entries with no error**. A chain that never reads content produces nothing, quietly. It's now a prologue in both modes, prepended unless the caller places it, because verbatim exists to break order and routing, not to run against unread files.

**Mode confusion needed to be loud.** `-Processors` without `-RunVerbatim` is refused with a message naming `-IncludeProcessors`; `-RunVerbatim` with no chain is refused too. And the three cautions moved inside verbatim — a test now pins that the same omission is silent under the sequencer (where placement is a compiler guarantee) and loud under verbatim.

I also moved `user.tests` onto the new surface rather than preserving the old one. What those tests promise is *the comment leaves and the code stays* — unchanged. The surface they used to reach it was implementation shape.

**The doc** (`d1d0645`) — [docs/sequencing-and-routing.md](utils/reposnapshot/reposnapshot-v3/docs/sequencing-and-routing.md), indexed in place of the archived entry. It describes what shipped rather than a target: the two-layer contract, the sequencer schema with its seven load-time rules, one-walk chain resolution, interning as a cache with opaque ordinal ids, pass-through as a compiled chain, dispatch, both caller modes, and the capability-named trail. Nine invariants, each earned during the build — including the three that cost the most to learn: pass-through is always compiled, chain ids are identities nothing may parse, and ordering is declared rather than inferred from file layout.

Remaining loose ends, both small: your [issues/…/sequencing-routing.md](issues/reposnapshot/design/sequencing-routing.md) still shows the pre-slot format in its code samples, and the cursor discussion could use a note recording the two departures (ordinals not guids, positional not `Compare-Object`) so nobody re-derives them.
